The **Ottoman Empire**,[^cite_note-37] also known historically as **Turkey**[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] or the **Turkish Empire**, was a state that spanned much of [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe), [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia), and [North Africa](21714.md) from the 14th century to the early 20th century, centred in modern-day [Turkey](11125639.md). It also controlled parts of southeastern [Central Europe](5188.md) between the early 16th and early 18th centuries.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43]

The empire emerged from a [*beylik*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_beyliks), or [principality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality), founded in northwestern [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) in c. 1299 by the [Turkoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkoman_\(ethnonym\)) tribal leader [Osman I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_I). His successors [conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_wars_in_Europe) much of Anatolia and expanded into the [Balkans](4829.md) by the mid-14th century, transforming their [petty kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petty_kingdom) into a transcontinental empire. The Ottomans ended the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) with the [conquest of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) in 1453 by [Mehmed II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II). [Further conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Mamluk_War_\(1516%E2%80%931517\)) by [Selim I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I) led the Sultans to adopt the title of [caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate). With its capital at [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Istanbul#Ottoman_Empire) and control over a significant portion of the [Mediterranean Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Basin), the Ottoman Empire was at the centre of interactions between the Middle East and Europe for six centuries. Ruling over diverse peoples, the empire granted varying levels of autonomy to its many confessional communities, or *[millets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\))*, to manage their own affairs per [Islamic law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_law). During the reign of [Suleiman the Magnificent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent) in the 16th century, the Ottoman Empire became a [global power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power).[^cite_note-44]

Modern academic consensus posits that the empire began to decline after defeat in the [Second Siege of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna) in 1683, but continued to maintain a flexible and strong economy, society and military into much of the 18th century. The Ottoman Empire fell behind technologically from the rest of Europe by the late 18th century as imperial authority fragmented. Further defeats from [Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire) and [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) culminated in the [loss of territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_and_modernization_of_the_Ottoman_Empire), and with [rising nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_nationalism_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) after the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution), a number of new states emerged in the Balkans. Following [Mahmud II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_II)'s reign and the *[Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat)* reforms over the course of the 19th century, the Ottoman state became more powerful and organised internally as a new [Ottoman identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottomanism) took hold. In the [1876 revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1876_Ottoman_Revolution), the Ottoman Empire attempted [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Constitutional_Era), before reverting to an [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy) under [Abdul Hamid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_II), following the [Great Eastern Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Eastern_Crisis).

Over the course of the late 19th century, Ottoman intellectuals known as [Young Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turks) sought to liberalise and rationalise society and politics along Western lines, culminating in the [Young Turk Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turk_Revolution) of 1908 led by the [Committee of Union and Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Union_and_Progress) (CUP), which reestablished [a constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Constitutional_Era). However, following the disastrous [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars), the CUP became increasingly radicalised and embraced [Turkish nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_nationalism), [leading to a coup d'état in 1913](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1913_Ottoman_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) that established a dictatorship.

In the 19th and in the start of 20th centuries, [persecution of Muslims during the Ottoman contraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims_during_the_Ottoman_contraction) and [in the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims#Russian_Empire) resulted in large-scale loss of life and [mass migration into modern-day Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhacir) from the [Balkans](4829.md), [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus), and [Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate).[^cite_note-45] The CUP joined [World War I](4764461.md) on the side of the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers). It struggled with internal dissent, especially the [Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt), and engaged in [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Ottoman_genocides) against [Armenians](140376.md), [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_genocide), and [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide). In the [aftermath of World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_World_War_I), the victorious [Allied Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) occupied and [partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) the Ottoman Empire, which lost its southern territories to the [United Kingdom and France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sykes%E2%80%93Picot_Agreement). The successful [Turkish War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_War_of_Independence), led by [Mustafa Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk) against the occupying Allies, led to the [end of the Ottoman sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolition_of_the_Ottoman_sultanate) in 1922.

## Etymology

See also: [Osman I § Name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_I#Name)

The word *Ottoman* is an historical [anglicisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicisation_\(linguistics\)) of the name of [Osman I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_I), the founder of the Empire and of the ruling [House of Osman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_dynasty) (also known as the Ottoman dynasty). Osman's name in turn was the Turkish form of the Arabic name *[ʿUthmān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uthman_\(name\))* (عثمان). In [Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish), the empire was referred to as *Devlet-i ʿAlīye-yi ʿOsmānīye* (دولت عليه عثمانیه), lit. 'Sublime Ottoman State', or simply *Devlet-i ʿOsmānīye* (دولت عثمانيه‎), lit. 'Ottoman State'.

The Turkish word for *Ottoman* (*Osmanlı*) originally referred to the tribal followers of Osman in the 14th century. The word subsequently came to be used to refer to the empire's military-administrative elite. In contrast, the term *Turk* (*Türk*) was used to refer to the Anatolian peasant and tribal population and was seen as a disparaging term when applied to urban, educated individuals.[^cite_note-46]<sup>: 26 </sup>[^cite_note-47] In the [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period), an educated, urban-dwelling Turkish speaker who was not a member of the military-administrative class typically referred to himself neither as an *Osmanlı* nor as a *Türk*, but rather as a *Rūmī* (رومى), or "Roman", meaning an inhabitant of the territory of the former [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) in the Balkans and Anatolia. The term *Rūmī* was also used to refer to Turkish speakers by the other Muslim peoples of the empire and beyond.[^cite_note-48]<sup>: 11 </sup>

In Western Europe, the names *Ottoman Empire*, *Turkish Empire* and *Turkey* were often used interchangeably, with *Turkey* being increasingly favoured both in formal and informal situations. This dichotomy was officially ended in 1920–1923, when the newly established [Ankara](802.md)-based [Turkish government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey) chose Turkey as the sole official name.

## History

Main article: [History of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)

See also: [Territorial evolution of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)

### Rise (c. 1299–1453)

Main article: [Rise of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



[Ottoman miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature) of [Osman I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_I) by Yahya Bustanzâde (18th century)

As the [Rum Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Rum) declined in the 13th century, [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) was a patchwork of independent Turkish principalities known as the [Anatolian Beyliks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_Beyliks). One of these, in the [Bithynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bithynia) region on the Byzantine frontier, was led by[^cite_note-49] Osman I (d. 1323/4),[^cite_note-50] a figure of obscure origin from whom the name Ottoman is derived.[^cite_note-51]<sup>: 444 </sup> Osman's early followers were Turkish tribal groups and Byzantine renegades, not all of them Muslim.[^cite_note-52]<sup>: 59 </sup>[^cite_note-53]<sup>: 127 </sup> Osman extended his principality by conquering Byzantine towns along the [Sakarya River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakarya_River), and a Byzantine defeat at the [Battle of Bapheus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bapheus) in 1302 contributed to his rise. It is not well understood how the early Ottomans came to dominate their neighbours, due to a lack of sources. The [Ghaza thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaza_thesis) popular during the 20th century credited their success to rallying religious warriors, but the idea is no longer generally accepted, and no other hypothesis has attracted broad support.[^cite_note-54]<sup>: 5, 10 </sup>[^cite_note-55]<sup>: 104 </sup>

In the century after Osman I, Ottoman rule began to extend over Anatolia and the [Balkans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Balkans). The [Byzantine–Ottoman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Ottoman_wars), waged in Anatolia in the late 13th century before entering Europe in the mid-14th century, were followed by the [Bulgarian–Ottoman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian%E2%80%93Ottoman_wars) and the [Serbian–Ottoman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian%E2%80%93Ottoman_wars) in the mid-14th century. Osman's son, [Orhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orhan), captured the northwestern Anatolian city of [Bursa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bursa) in 1326 and made it the new capital, supplanting Byzantine control in the region. The important port of [Thessaloniki](40471.md) was captured from the [Venetians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice) in 1387 and sacked. The Ottoman victory in [Kosovo in 1389](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo) effectively marked the [end of Serbian power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Serbian_Empire) in the region, paving the way for Ottoman expansion into Europe.[^cite_note-56]<sup>: 95–96 </sup> The [Battle of Nicopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Nicopolis) for the [Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Bulgarian_Empire) [Tsardom of Vidin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Vidin) in 1396, regarded as the last large-scale [crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) of the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), also failed to stop the Ottomans.[^cite_note-57]



The [Battle of Nicopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Nicopolis) in 1396, as depicted in an [Ottoman miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature) from 1523

As the Turks expanded into the Balkans, the [conquest of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sieges_of_Constantinople#Ottoman_Sieges) became a crucial objective. The Ottomans already controlled nearly all former Byzantine lands surrounding the city, but Constantinople's defenses and strategic position on the [Bosporus](3705.md) Strait made it difficult to conquer. In 1402, the Byzantines were temporarily relieved when the [Turco-Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turco-Mongol_tradition) leader [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur), founder of the [Timurid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Empire), invaded Ottoman Anatolia from the east. In the [Battle of Ankara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ankara) in 1402, Timur defeated Ottoman forces and took Sultan [Bayezid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayezid_I) as prisoner, throwing the empire into disorder. The [ensuing civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Interregnum) lasted from 1402 to 1413 as Bayezid's sons fought over succession. It ended when [Mehmed I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_I) emerged as the sultan.[^cite_note-58]<sup>: 363 </sup>

The Balkan territories lost by the Ottomans after 1402, including Thessaloniki, Macedonia, and Kosovo, were recovered by [Murad II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murad_II) between the 1430s and 1450s. On 10 November 1444, Murad repelled the [Crusade of Varna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusade_of_Varna) by defeating the Hungarian, Polish, and [Wallachian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia) armies under [Władysław III of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_III_of_Poland) and [John Hunyadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hunyadi) at the [Battle of Varna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Varna), although Albanians under [Skanderbeg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg) continued to resist. Four years later, John Hunyadi prepared another army of Hungarian and Wallachian forces to attack the Turks, but was again defeated at the [Second Battle of Kosovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo_\(1448\)) in 1448.[^cite_note-59]<sup>: 29 </sup>

In modern historiography, there is a direct connection between the rapid Ottoman advances and the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) from the mid-14th century onwards. Byzantine territories were exhausted demographically and militarily due to the plague, and this facilitated Ottoman expansion. In addition, slave hunting was a main economic motivation for the Ottomans. Some 21st-century authors re-periodise conquest of the Balkans into the *akıncı phase*, which spanned 8 to 13 decades, characterised by continuous slave hunting and destruction, followed by administrative integration into the Empire.[^cite_note-Schmitt-Kiprovska-60][^cite_note-Nükhet_Varlik-61][^cite_note-Ayalon-2014-62][^cite_note-63]

### Expansion and peak (1453–1566)

Main article: [Classical Age of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Age_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



Sultan [Mehmed the Conqueror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II)'s entry into [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople); painting by [Fausto Zonaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fausto_Zonaro) (1854–1929)

The son of Murad II, [Mehmed the Conqueror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_the_Conqueror), reorganised both state and military, and on 29 May 1453 conquered [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople), ending the Byzantine Empire.[^cite_note-Quataert2005-64] Mehmed allowed the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md) to maintain its autonomy and land in exchange for accepting Ottoman authority.[^cite_note-books.google-65] Due to tension between the states of western Europe and the later Byzantine Empire, most of the Orthodox population accepted Ottoman rule, as preferable to Venetian rule.[^cite_note-books.google-65] [Albanian resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_uprisings_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) was a major obstacle to Ottoman expansion on the Italian peninsula.[^cite_note-66]

In the 15th and 16th centuries, the Ottoman Empire entered a [period of expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_of_the_Ottoman_Empire). The Empire prospered under the rule of a line of committed and effective [Sultans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Dynasty). It flourished economically due to its control of the major overland trade routes between Europe and Asia.[^cite_note-67]<sup>: 111 </sup>[^cite_note-68]

Sultan [Selim I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I) (1512–1520) dramatically expanded the eastern and southern frontiers by defeating [Shah Ismail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_I) of [Safavid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran), in the [Battle of Chaldiran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Chaldiran).[^cite_note-69]<sup>: 91–105 </sup> Selim I established [Ottoman rule in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_Eyalet) by defeating and annexing the [Mamluk Sultanate of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate) and created a naval presence on the [Red Sea](26191.md). After this Ottoman expansion, competition began between the [Portuguese Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) and the Ottomans to become the dominant power in the region.[^cite_note-70]<sup>: 55–76 </sup>





[Suleiman the Magnificent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent) and his wife [Hürrem Sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BCrrem_Sultan), by 16th-century [Venetian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice) [painter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_painting) [Titian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titian)

[Suleiman the Magnificent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent) (1520–1566)[^cite_note-71] captured [Belgrade](55904.md) in 1521, conquered the southern and central parts of the [Kingdom of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary) as part of the [Ottoman–Hungarian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Hungarian_Wars), and, after his historic victory in the [Battle of Mohács](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Moh%C3%A1cs) in 1526, he established Ottoman rule in the territory of present-day Hungary and other Central European territories. He then laid [siege to Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Vienna_\(1529\)) in 1529, but failed to take the city.[^cite_note-72]<sup>: 50 </sup> In 1532, he made another [attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg%E2%80%93Ottoman_wars_in_Hungary_\(1526%E2%80%931568\)) on Vienna, but was repulsed in the [siege of Güns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_G%C3%BCns).[^cite_note-Thompson442-73][^cite_note-Ágoston_and_Alan_Masters583-74] [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania), Wallachia and, intermittently, [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia), became tributary principalities of the Empire. In the east, the Ottoman Turks took [Baghdad](4492.md) from the Persians in 1535, establishing [Ottoman rule in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Iraq) and gaining naval access to the [Persian Gulf](24761.md). After the end of the [Ottoman–Safavid War (1532–1555)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Safavid_War_\(1532%E2%80%931555\)), the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) became partitioned for the first time between the Safavids and the Ottomans, a *[status quo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_quo)* that persisted until the [Russian conquest of the Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_conquest_of_the_Caucasus) in the 19th century. By this partitioning as signed in the [Peace of Amasya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Amasya), [Western Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Armenia), western [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan), and [Western Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Imereti) fell into Ottoman hands,[^cite_note-75] while southern [Dagestan](751099.md), [Eastern Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Armenia), [Eastern Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Georgia_\(country\)), and [Azerbaijan](746.md) remained Persian.[^cite_note-76]



Ottoman miniature of the [Battle of Mohács](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Moh%C3%A1cs) in 1526

[^cite_note-77]

In 1539, a 60,000-strong Ottoman army besieged the [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_Spain) garrison of [Castelnuovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Castelnuovo) on the [Adriatic coast](23275478.md); the successful siege cost the Ottomans 8,000 casualties,[^cite_note-78] but [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice) agreed to terms in 1540, surrendering most of its empire in the [Aegean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea) and the [Morea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morea). [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Modern_France) and the Ottoman Empire, united by mutual opposition to [Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg) rule,[^cite_note-AksanOW-79] became allies. The French conquests of [Nice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Nice) (1543) and [Corsica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Corsica_\(1553\)) (1553) occurred as a joint venture between French king [Francis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_I_of_France) and Suleiman, and were commanded by the Ottoman admirals [Hayreddin Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayreddin_Barbarossa) and [Dragut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragut).[^cite_note-80] France supported the Ottomans with an artillery unit during the 1543 Ottoman [conquest of Esztergom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Esztergom_\(1543\)) in northern Hungary. After further advances by the Turks, the Habsburg ruler [Ferdinand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor) officially recognised Ottoman ascendancy in Hungary in 1547. Suleiman died of natural causes during the [siege of Szigetvár](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Szigetv%C3%A1r) in 1566. Following his death, the Ottomans were said to be declining, although this has been rejected by many scholars.[^cite_note-decline-81] By the end of Suleiman's reign, the Empire spanned approximately 877,888 mi<sup>2</sup> (2,273,720 km<sup>2</sup>), extending over three continents.[^cite_note-82]<sup>: 545 </sup>



Ottoman admiral [Barbarossa Hayreddin Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayreddin_Barbarossa) defeated the [Holy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_League_\(1538\)) of [Charles V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor) under the command of [Andrea Doria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrea_Doria) at the [Battle of Preveza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Preveza) in 1538.

The Empire became a dominant naval force, controlling much of the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md).[^cite_note-83]<sup>: 61 </sup> The Empire was now a major part of European politics. The Ottomans became involved in multi-continental religious wars when Spain and Portugal were united under the [Iberian Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Union). The Ottomans were holders of the Caliph title, meaning they were the leaders of Muslims worldwide. The Iberians were leaders of the Christian crusaders, and so the two fought in a worldwide conflict. There were zones of operations in the Mediterranean[^cite_note-84] and [Indian Ocean](14580.md),[^cite_note-85] where Iberians circumnavigated Africa to reach India and, on their way, wage war upon the Ottomans and their local Muslim allies. Likewise, the Iberians passed through newly-Christianised [Latin America](18524.md) and [had sent expeditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Salcedo) that traversed the Pacific to Christianise the formerly Muslim Philippines and use it as a base to attack the Muslims in the [Far East](11672.md).[^cite_note-86] In this case, the Ottomans [sent armies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_expeditions_to_Aceh) to aid its easternmost vassal and territory, the [Sultanate of Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Aceh) in Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-87]<sup>: 84 </sup>[^cite_note-88]

The [Spanish–Ottoman wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93Ottoman_wars) took place primarily in the Mediterranean. [Barbary corsairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbary_corsairs) from the North African cities of Algiers, Tunis, and Tripoli, which were nominally part of the Ottoman Empire, captured thousands of merchant ships and raided coastal towns in Europe, [enslaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbary_slave_trade) the people they captured.[^cite_note-Toll-89]

During the 1600s, the world conflict between the Ottoman Caliphate and Iberian Union was a stalemate since [both were at similar population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_population_in_1600), technology and economic levels. Nevertheless, the success of the Ottoman political and military establishment was compared to the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), despite the difference in size, by the likes of contemporary Italian scholar [Francesco Sansovino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesco_Sansovino) and French political philosopher [Jean Bodin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Bodin).[^cite_note-deringil709-90]

### Stagnation and reform (1566–1827)

#### Revolts, reversals, and revivals (1566–1683)

Main article: [Transformation of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transformation_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)

Further information: [Ottoman decline thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_decline_thesis)



Late 16th or early 17th century [Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Navy) [galley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galley) known as *[Tarihi Kadırga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarihi_Kad%C4%B1rga)* at the [Istanbul Naval Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Naval_Museum), built in the period between the reigns of Sultan [Murad III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murad_III) (1574–1595) and Sultan [Mehmed IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_IV) (1648–1687)

[^cite_note-kadirga1-91]

[^cite_note-kadirga2-92]

In the second half of the 16th century, the Ottoman Empire came under increasing strain from inflation and the rapidly rising costs of warfare that were impacting both Europe and the Middle East.[^cite_note-93] These pressures led to a series of crises around the year 1600, placing great strain upon the Ottoman system of government.[^cite_note-94]<sup>: 413–414 </sup> The empire underwent a series of transformations of its political and military institutions in response to these challenges, enabling it to successfully adapt to the new conditions of the 17th century and remain powerful, both militarily and economically.[^cite_note-decline-81][^cite_note-95]<sup>: 10 </sup> Historians of the mid-20th century once characterised this period as one of stagnation and decline, but this view is now rejected by the majority of academics.[^cite_note-decline-81]

The discovery of new maritime trade routes by Western European states allowed them to avoid the Ottoman trade monopoly. The [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal) discovery of the [Cape of Good Hope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_of_Good_Hope) in 1488 initiated [a series of Ottoman-Portuguese naval wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_naval_expeditions_in_the_Indian_Ocean) in the [Indian Ocean](14580.md) throughout the 16th century. Despite the growing European presence in the Indian Ocean, Ottoman trade with the east continued to flourish. Cairo, in particular, benefitted from the rise of Yemeni coffee as a popular consumer commodity. As coffeehouses appeared in cities and towns across the empire, Cairo developed into a major centre for its trade, contributing to its continued prosperity throughout the seventeenth and much of the 18th century.[^cite_note-96]<sup>: 507–508 </sup>

Under [Ivan IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_IV) (1533–1584), the [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia) expanded into the Volga and Caspian regions at the expense of the [Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars) [khanates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate). In 1571, the Crimean khan [Devlet I Giray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devlet_I_Giray), commanded by the Ottomans, [invaded Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Crimean_Wars) and [burned Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_of_Moscow_\(1571\)).[^cite_note-Davies2007-97] The next year, the invasion was repeated but repelled at the [Battle of Molodi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Molodi). The Ottoman Empire and its vassal, the Crimean Khanate, continued to invade Eastern Europe in a series of [slave raids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean%E2%80%93Nogai_slave_raids_in_Eastern_Europe),[^cite_note-Subtelny2000-98] and remained a significant power in Eastern Europe until the end of the 17th century.[^cite_note-99] The Crimean cavalry became indispensable for the Ottomans' campaigns against Russia, [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish-Lithuanian_Commonwealth), [Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary_\(1526%E2%80%931867\)), and [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran).[^cite_note-100]



Order of battle of the two fleets in the [Battle of Lepanto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Lepanto), with an allegory of the three powers of the [Holy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_League_\(1571\)) in the foreground, fresco by [Giorgio Vasari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgio_Vasari)

The Ottomans decided to conquer [Venetian Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_Cyprus) and on 22 July 1570, Nicosia was besieged; 50,000 Christians died, and 180,000 were enslaved.[^cite_note-101]<sup>: 67 </sup> On 15 September 1570, the Ottoman cavalry appeared before the last Venetian stronghold in Cyprus, Famagusta. The Venetian defenders held out for 11 months against a force that at its peak numbered 200,000 men with 145 cannons; 163,000 cannonballs struck the walls of Famagusta before it fell to the Ottomans in August 1571. The [Siege of Famagusta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Famagusta) claimed 50,000 Ottoman casualties.[^cite_note-102]<sup>: 328 </sup> Meanwhile, the [Holy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_League_\(1571\)) consisting of mostly Spanish and Venetian fleets won a victory over the Ottoman fleet at the [Battle of Lepanto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Lepanto) (1571), off southwestern Greece; Catholic forces killed over 30,000 Turks and destroyed 200 of their ships.[^cite_note-103]<sup>: 24 </sup> It was a startling, if mostly symbolic,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979272-104] blow to the image of Ottoman invincibility, an image which the victory of the [Knights of Malta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Hospitaller) over the Ottoman invaders in the 1565 [siege of Malta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Siege_of_Malta) had recently set about eroding.[^cite_note-105] The battle was far more damaging to the Ottoman navy in sapping experienced manpower than the loss of ships, which were rapidly replaced.[^cite_note-106]<sup>: 53 </sup> The Ottoman navy recovered quickly, persuading Venice to sign a peace treaty in 1573, allowing the Ottomans to expand and consolidate their position in North Africa.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198067-107]

By contrast, the [Habsburg frontier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Habsburg_wars) had settled somewhat, a stalemate caused by a stiffening of the [Habsburg defences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Frontier).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198071-108] The [Long Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Turkish_War) against Habsburg Austria (1593–1606) created the need for greater numbers of Ottoman infantry equipped with firearms, resulting in a relaxation of recruitment policy. This contributed to problems of indiscipline and outright rebelliousness within the corps, which were never fully solved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198090–92-109] Irregular sharpshooters ([Sekban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sekban)) were also recruited, and on demobilisation turned to [brigandage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigandage) in the [Celali rebellions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celali_rebellions) (1590–1610), which engendered widespread anarchy in [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) in the late 16th and early 17th centuries.[^cite_note-110]<sup>: 24 </sup> With the Empire's population reaching 30 million people by 1600, the shortage of land placed further pressure on the government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979281-111] In spite of these problems, the Ottoman state remained strong, and its army did not collapse or suffer crushing defeats. The only exceptions were campaigns against the [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty) of Persia, where many of the Ottoman eastern provinces were lost, some permanently. This [1603–1618 war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Safavid_War_\(1603%E2%80%931618\)) eventually resulted in the [Treaty of Nasuh Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Nasuh_Pasha), which ceded the entire Caucasus, except westernmost Georgia, back into the possession of [Safavid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran).[^cite_note-112] The treaty ending the [Cretan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretan_War_\(1645%E2%80%931669\)) cost Venice much of [Dalmatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_Dalmatia), its Aegean island possessions, and [Crete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Candia).[^cite_note-113]<sup>: 33 </sup>

During his brief majority reign, [Murad IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murad_IV) (1623–1640) reasserted central authority and [recaptured Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Safavid_War_\(1623%E2%80%931639\)) (1639) from the Safavids.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198073-114] The resulting [Treaty of Zuhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Zuhab) of that same year decisively divided the Caucasus and adjacent regions between the two neighbouring empires as it had already been defined in the 1555 Peace of Amasya.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116]

The [Sultanate of Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Women) (1533–1656) was a period during which imperial women, especially the consorts and mothers of sultans, exercised significant political influence in the Ottoman Empire. The most prominent women of this period were [Hürrem Sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BCrrem_Sultan), [Kösem Sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6sem_Sultan) and her daughter-in-law [Turhan Hatice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turhan_Hatice), whose political rivalry culminated in Kösem's murder in 1651.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198074–75-117] During the [Köprülü era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6pr%C3%BCl%C3%BC_era) (1656–1703), effective control of the Empire was exercised by a sequence of [grand viziers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_vizier) from the Köprülü family. The Köprülü Vizierate saw renewed military success with authority restored in Transylvania, the conquest of [Crete](6591.md) completed in 1669, and [expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Ottoman_War_\(1672%E2%80%931676\)) into [Polish southern Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Ukraine#Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), with the strongholds of [Khotyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khotyn), and [Kamianets-Podilskyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamianets-Podilskyi) and the territory of [Podolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podolia) ceding to an Ottoman controlled [Podolia Eyalet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podolia_Eyalet) in 1676.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198080–81-118]



The [Second Siege of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna) in 1683, by [Frans Geffels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frans_Geffels) (1624–1694)

This period of renewed assertiveness came to a calamitous end in 1683 when Grand Vizier [Kara Mustafa Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_Mustafa_Pasha) led a huge army to attempt a second Ottoman siege of [Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Vienna) in the [Great Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Turkish_War) of 1683–1699. The final assault being fatally delayed, the Ottoman forces were swept away by allied Habsburg, German, and Polish forces spearheaded by the Polish king [John III Sobieski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_III_Sobieski) at the [Battle of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna). The alliance of the [Holy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_League_\(1684\)) pressed home the advantage of the defeat at Vienna, culminating in the [Treaty of Karlowitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Karlowitz) (26 January 1699), which ended the Great Turkish War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979357-119] The Ottomans surrendered control of significant territories, many permanently.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198084-120] [Mustafa II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_II) (1695–1703) led the counterattack of 1695–1696 against the Habsburgs in Hungary, but was undone at the disastrous defeat at [Zenta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Zenta) (in modern Serbia), 11 September 1697.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198083–84-121]

#### Military defeats

Aside from the loss of the [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat) and the temporary loss of [Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Belgrade) (1717–1739), the Ottoman border on the [Danube](21209639.md) and [Sava](587845.md) remained stable during the 18th century. [Russian expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_of_Russia_1500%E2%80%931800), however, presented a large and growing threat.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979371-122] Accordingly, King [Charles XII of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_XII_of_Sweden) was welcomed as an ally in the Ottoman Empire following his defeat by the Russians at the [Battle of Poltava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Poltava) of 1709 in central Ukraine (part of the [Great Northern War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Northern_War) of 1700–1721).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979371-122] Charles XII persuaded the Ottoman Sultan [Ahmed III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_III) to declare war on Russia, which resulted in an Ottoman victory in the [Pruth River Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pruth_River_Campaign) of 1710–1711, in Moldavia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979372-123]



Austrian troops led by [Prince Eugene of Savoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Eugene_of_Savoy) capture [Belgrade](55904.md) in 1717. Austrian control in Serbia lasted until the Turkish victory in the [Austro-Russian–Turkish War (1735–1739)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1735%E2%80%931739\)). With the 1739 [Treaty of Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Belgrade), the Ottoman Empire regained northern [Bosnia](3463.md), [Habsburg Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia_\(1718%E2%80%931739\)) (including Belgrade), [Oltenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oltenia) and the southern parts of the [Banat of Temeswar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat_of_Temeswar).

After the [Austro-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Turkish_War_\(1716%E2%80%931718\)), the [Treaty of Passarowitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Passarowitz) confirmed the loss of the Banat, Serbia, and "Little Walachia" ([Oltenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oltenia)) to Austria. The Treaty also revealed that the Ottoman Empire was on the defensive and unlikely to present any further aggression in Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979376-124] The [Austro-Russian–Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Russian%E2%80%93Turkish_War_\(1735%E2%80%931739\)) (1735–1739), which was ended by the [Treaty of Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Belgrade) in 1739, resulted in the Ottoman recovery of northern [Bosnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnia), [Habsburg Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia_\(1718%E2%80%931739\)) (including Belgrade), Oltenia and the southern parts of the [Banat of Temeswar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat_of_Temeswar); but the Empire lost the port of [Azov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azov), north of the Crimean Peninsula, to the Russians. After this treaty the Ottoman Empire was able to enjoy a generation of peace in Europe, as Austria and Russia were forced to deal with the rise of [Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979392-125]

[Educational and technological reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_Technology_in_the_Ottoman_Empire#Education) came about, including the establishment of higher education institutions such as the [Istanbul Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Technical_University).[^cite_note-126] In 1734 an artillery school was established to impart Western-style artillery methods, but the Islamic clergy successfully objected under the grounds of [theodicy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodicy).[^cite_note-books.google_a-127] In 1754 the artillery school was reopened on a semi-secret basis.[^cite_note-books.google_a-127] In 1726, [Ibrahim Muteferrika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Muteferrika) convinced the Grand Vizier [Nevşehirli Damat Ibrahim Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nev%C5%9Fehirli_Damat_Ibrahim_Pasha), the [Grand Mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mufti), and the clergy on the efficiency of the printing press, and Muteferrika was later granted by Sultan Ahmed III permission to publish non-religious books (despite opposition from some [calligraphers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calligraphy) and religious leaders).[^cite_note-katip_celebi-128] Muteferrika's press published its first book in 1729 and, by 1743, issued 17 works in 23 volumes, each having between 500 and 1,000 copies.[^cite_note-katip_celebi-128][^cite_note-watson-129]

In North Africa, Spain [conquered Oran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_conquest_of_Oran_\(1732\)) from the autonomous [Deylik of Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deylik_of_Algiers). The [Bey of Oran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beylik_of_Oran) received an army from Algiers, but it failed to recapture [Oran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oran); the siege caused the deaths of 1,500 Spaniards, and even more Algerians. The Spanish also massacred many Muslim soldiers.[^cite_note-130] In 1792, Spain abandoned Oran, selling it to the Deylik of Algiers.



Ottoman troops attempting to halt the advancing Russians during the [Siege of Ochakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Ochakov_\(1788\)) in 1788

In 1768 Russian-backed Ukrainian [Haidamakas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haidamaka), pursuing Polish confederates, entered [Balta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balta,_Ukraine), an Ottoman-controlled town on the border of Bessarabia in Ukraine, massacred its citizens, and burned the town to the ground. This action provoked the Ottoman Empire into the [Russo-Turkish War of 1768–1774](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1768%E2%80%931774\)). The [Treaty of Küçük Kaynarca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_K%C3%BC%C3%A7%C3%BCk_Kaynarca) of 1774 ended the war and provided freedom of worship for the Christian citizens of the Ottoman-controlled provinces of Wallachia and Moldavia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979405-131] By the late 18th century, after a number of defeats in the wars with Russia, some people in the Ottoman Empire began to conclude that the reforms of [Peter the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great) had given the Russians an edge, and the Ottomans would have to keep up with Western technology in order to avoid further defeats.[^cite_note-books.google_a-127]



[Selim III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_III) receiving dignitaries during an audience at the Gate of Felicity, [Topkapı Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkap%C4%B1_Palace). Painting by [Konstantin Kapıdağlı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Kap%C4%B1da%C4%9Fl%C4%B1).

[Selim III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_III) (1789–1807) made the first major attempts to [modernise the army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_military_reform_efforts), but his reforms were hampered by the religious leadership and the [Janissary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janissary) corps. Jealous of their privileges and firmly opposed to change, the Janissary [revolted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janissary_revolts). Selim's efforts cost him his throne and his life, but were resolved in spectacular and bloody fashion by his successor, the dynamic [Mahmud II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_II), who [eliminated the Janissary corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auspicious_Incident) in 1826.



The siege of the Acropolis in 1826–1827 during the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence)

The [Serbian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_revolution) (1804–1815) marked the beginning of an era of [national awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_nationalism_under_the_Ottoman_Empire) in the [Balkans](4829.md) during the [Eastern Question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Question). In 1811, the fundamentalist Wahhabis of Arabia, led by the al-Saud family, revolted against the Ottomans. Unable to defeat the Wahhabi rebels, the Sublime Porte had [Muhammad Ali Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt) of [Kavala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kavala), the *[vali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali)* (governor) of the [Eyalet of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_Eyalet), tasked with retaking Arabia, which ended with the destruction of the [Emirate of Diriyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Diriyah) in 1818. The [suzerainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzerainty) of Serbia as a hereditary monarchy under its own [dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obrenovi%C4%87_dynasty) was acknowledged *[de jure](43594.md)* in 1830.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-Berend2003-133] In 1821, the [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Greece) [declared war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence) on the Sultan. A rebellion that originated in Moldavia as a diversion was followed by the main revolution in the [Peloponnese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnese), which, along with the northern part of the [Gulf of Corinth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Corinth), became the first parts of the Ottoman Empire to achieve independence (in 1829). In 1830, the French invaded the [Deylik of Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deylik_of_Algiers). [The campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Algiers_in_1830) took 21 days, and resulted in over 5,000 Algerian military casualties,[^cite_note-De_Quatrebarbes-1831-134] and about 2,600 French ones.[^cite_note-De_Quatrebarbes-1831-134][^cite_note-135] Before the French invasion the total population of Algeria was most likely between 3,000,000 and 5,000,000.[^cite_note-136] By 1873, the population of Algeria (excluding several hundred thousand newly arrived French settlers) had decreased to 2,172,000.[^cite_note-137] In 1831, Muhammad Ali of Egypt revolted against Sultan [Mahmud II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_II) due to the latter's refusal to grant him the governorships of [Greater Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(region\)) and [Crete](6591.md), which the Sultan had promised him in exchange for sending military assistance to put down the [Greek revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Revolt) (1821–1829) that ultimately ended with the formal [independence of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Protocol_\(1830\)) in 1830. It was a costly enterprise for Muhammad Ali, who had lost his fleet at the [Battle of Navarino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Navarino) in 1827. Thus began the first [Egyptian–Ottoman War (1831–1833)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian%E2%80%93Ottoman_War_\(1831%E2%80%931833\)), during which the French-trained army of Muhammad Ali, under the command of his son [Ibrahim Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Pasha_of_Egypt), defeated the Ottoman Army as it marched into [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia), reaching the city of [Kütahya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%BCtahya) within 320 km (200 mi) of the capital, Constantinople.[^cite_note-Effraim-138]<sup>: 95 </sup> In desperation, Sultan Mahmud II appealed to the empire's traditional arch-rival Russia for help, asking Emperor [Nicholas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_I_of_Russia) to send an expeditionary force to assist him.[^cite_note-Effraim-138]<sup>: 96 </sup> In return for signing the [Treaty of Hünkâr İskelesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_H%C3%BCnk%C3%A2r_%C4%B0skelesi), the Russians sent the expeditionary force which deterred Ibrahim Pasha from marching any further towards Constantinople.[^cite_note-Effraim-138]<sup>: 96 </sup> Under the terms of the [Convention of Kütahya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_of_K%C3%BCtahya), signed on 5 May 1833, Muhammad Ali agreed to abandon his campaign against the Sultan, in exchange for which he was made the *vali* (governor) of the *[vilayets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilayet)* (provinces) of Crete, [Aleppo](159244.md), [Tripoli](268796.md), [Damascus](8914.md) and [Sidon](28366.md) (the latter four comprising modern [Syria](7515849.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md)), and given the right to collect taxes in [Adana](185243.md).[^cite_note-Effraim-138]<sup>: 96 </sup> Had it not been for the Russian intervention, Sultan Mahmud II could have faced the risk of being overthrown and Muhammad Ali could have even become the new Sultan. These events marked the beginning of a recurring pattern where the Sublime Porte needed the help of foreign powers to protect itself.[^cite_note-Effraim-138]<sup>: 95–96 </sup>

In 1839, the [Sublime Porte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sublime_Porte) attempted to take back what it lost to the *[de facto](43594.md)* autonomous, but *[de jure](43594.md)* still Ottoman [Eyalet of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_Eyalet), but its forces were initially defeated, which led to the [Oriental Crisis of 1840](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Crisis_of_1840). Muhammad Ali had close relations with [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Monarchy), and the prospect of him becoming the Sultan of Egypt was widely viewed as putting the entire [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) into the French sphere of influence.[^cite_note-Effraim-138]<sup>: 96 </sup> As the Sublime Porte had proved itself incapable of defeating Muhammad Ali,[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140] the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) and [Austrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire) provided military assistance, and the second [Egyptian–Ottoman War (1839–1841)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian%E2%80%93Ottoman_War_\(1839%E2%80%931841\)) ended with Ottoman victory and the restoration of Ottoman suzerainty over Egypt Eyalet and the Levant.[^cite_note-Effraim-138]<sup>: 96 </sup>

By the mid-19th century, the Ottoman Empire was called the "[sick man of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sick_man_of_Europe)". Three suzerain states – the [Principality of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Serbia), [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia) and [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia) – moved towards *de jure* independence during the 1860s and 1870s.

### Decline and modernisation (1828–1908)

Main article: [Decline and modernization of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_and_modernization_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



Opening ceremony of the First [Ottoman Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Assembly_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) at the [Dolmabahçe Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmabah%C3%A7e_Palace) in 1876. The [First Constitutional Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Constitutional_Era) lasted only two years until 1878. The Ottoman Constitution and Parliament were [restored 30 years later](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Constitutional_Era) with the [Young Turk Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turk_Revolution) in 1908.

During the [Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat) period (1839–1876), the government's series of constitutional reforms led to a fairly modern [conscripted army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_the_Ottoman_Empire), banking system reforms, the decriminalisation of homosexuality, the replacement of religious law with secular law,[^cite_note-141] and guilds with modern factories. The Ottoman Ministry of Post was established in Istanbul in 1840. American inventor [Samuel Morse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Morse) received an Ottoman patent for the telegraph in 1847, issued by Sultan [Abdülmecid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd%C3%BClmecid_I), who personally tested the invention.[^cite_note-142] The reformist period peaked with the Constitution, called the *[Kanûn-u Esâsî](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kan%C3%BBn-u_Es%C3%A2s%C3%AE)*. The empire's [First Constitutional era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Constitutional_Era_\(Ottoman_Empire\)) was short-lived. The parliament survived for only two years before the sultan suspended it.

The empire's Christian population, owing to their higher educational levels, started to pull ahead of the Muslim majority, leading to much resentment.[^cite_note-books.google_b-143] In 1861, there were 571 primary and 94 secondary schools for Ottoman Christians, with 140,000 pupils in total, a figure that vastly exceeded the number of Muslim children in school at the time, who were further hindered by the amount of time spent learning Arabic and Islamic theology.[^cite_note-books.google_b-143] Author Norman Stone suggests that the Arabic alphabet, in which Turkish was written [until 1928](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_alphabet_reform), was ill-suited to reflect the sounds of Turkish (which is a Turkic as opposed to Semitic language), which imposed further difficulty on Turkish children.[^cite_note-books.google_b-143] In turn, Christians' higher educational levels allowed them to play a larger role in the economy, with the rise in prominence of groups such as the [Sursock family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sursock_family) indicative of this.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-books.google_b-143] In 1911, of the 654 wholesale companies in Istanbul, 528 were owned by ethnic Greeks.[^cite_note-books.google_b-143] In many cases, Christians and Jews gained protection from European consuls and citizenship, meaning they were protected from Ottoman law and not subject to the same economic regulations as their Muslim counterparts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERogan201193-145]



Ottoman troops storming [Fort Shefketil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shekvetili) during the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War) of 1853–1856

The [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War) (1853–1856) was part of a long-running contest between the major European powers for influence over territories of the declining Ottoman Empire. The financial burden of the war led the Ottoman state to issue [foreign loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_public_debt) amounting to 5 million pounds sterling on 4 August 1854.[^cite_note-146]<sup>: 32 </sup>[^cite_note-147]<sup>: 71 </sup> The war caused an exodus of the [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars), about 200,000 of whom moved to the Ottoman Empire in continuing waves of emigration.[^cite_note-148]<sup>: 79–108 </sup> Toward the end of the [Caucasian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_War), 90% of the [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) were [ethnically cleansed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing_of_Circassians)[^cite_note-149] and exiled from their homelands in the Caucasus, fleeing to the Ottoman Empire,[^cite_note-150] resulting in the settlement of 500,000 to 700,000 Circassians in the Ottoman Empire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamed-Troyansky202449-151] Crimean Tatar refugees in the late 19th century played an especially notable role in seeking to modernise Ottoman education and in first promoting both [Pan-Turkism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Turkism) and a sense of Turkish nationalism.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-152]



The monarchs of Europe in [Paris](22989.md) ([Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III) is at the centre, Sultan [Abdulaziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulaziz) is second from right), fictional representation of the opening of the [Universal Exposition of 1867](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exposition_Universelle_\(1867\)).

In this period, the Ottoman Empire spent only small amounts of public funds on education; for example, in 1860–1861 only 0.2% of the total budget was invested in education.[^cite_note-153]<sup>: 50 </sup> As the Ottoman state attempted to modernise its infrastructure and army in response to outside threats, it opened itself up to a different kind of threat: that of creditors. As the historian Eugene Rogan has written, "the single greatest threat to the independence of the Middle East" in the 19th century "was not the armies of Europe but its banks".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERogan2011105-154] The Ottoman state, which had begun taking on debt with the Crimean War, was forced to declare bankruptcy in 1875.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERogan2011106-155] By 1881, the Ottoman Empire agreed to have its debt controlled by the [Ottoman Public Debt Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Public_Debt_Administration), a council of European men with presidency alternating between France and Britain. The body controlled swaths of the Ottoman economy, and used its position to ensure that European capital continued to penetrate the empire, often to the detriment of local Ottoman interests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERogan2011106-155]



The Ottoman Empire in 1875 under Sultan [Abdulaziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulaziz)

The Ottoman [bashi-bazouks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashi-bazouk) suppressed the [Bulgarian uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_Uprising) of 1876, massacring up to 100,000 people in the process.[^cite_note-156]<sup>: 139 </sup> The [Russo-Turkish War (1877–1878)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1877%E2%80%931878\)) ended with a decisive victory for Russia. As a result, Ottoman holdings in Europe declined sharply: [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Bulgaria) was established as an independent principality inside the Ottoman Empire; [Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Principalities) achieved full independence; and [Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Serbia) and [Montenegro](20760.md) finally gained complete independence, but with smaller territories. In 1878, [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) unilaterally occupied the Ottoman provinces of [Bosnia-Herzegovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnia_Vilayet) and [Novi Pazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjak_of_Novi_Pazar).

British Prime Minister [Benjamin Disraeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli) advocated restoring the Ottoman territories on the Balkan Peninsula during the [Congress of Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Berlin), and in return, Britain assumed the administration of [Cyprus](5593.md) in 1878.[^cite_note-157]<sup>: 228–254 </sup> Britain later sent troops to [Egypt](8087628.md) in 1882 to put down the [Urabi Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urabi_Revolt) (Sultan [Abdul Hamid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_II) was too paranoid to mobilise his own army, fearing this would result in a coup d'état), effectively gaining control in both territories. Abdul Hamid II was so fearful of a coup that he did not allow his army to conduct war games, lest this serve as cover for a coup, but he did see the need for military mobilisation. In 1883, a German military mission under General Baron [Colmar von der Goltz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colmar_Freiherr_von_der_Goltz) arrived to train the Ottoman Army, leading to the so-called "Goltz generation" of German-trained officers, who played a notable role in the politics of the empire's last years.[^cite_note-158]<sup>: 24 </sup>

From 1894 to 1896, between 100,000 and 300,000 Armenians living throughout the empire were killed in what became known as the [Hamidian massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamidian_massacres).[^cite_note-159]<sup>: 42 </sup>

In 1897 the population was 19 million, of whom 14 million (74%) were Muslim. An additional 20 million lived in provinces that remained under the sultan's nominal suzerainty but were entirely outside his actual power. One by one the Porte lost nominal authority. They included Egypt, Tunisia, Bulgaria, Cyprus, Bosnia-Herzegovina, and Lebanon.[^cite_note-160]

As the Ottoman Empire gradually shrank, 7–9 million Muslims from its former territories in the Caucasus, [Crimea](163045.md), Balkans, and the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean) islands migrated to Anatolia and [Eastern Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Thrace).[^cite_note-Karpat2004-161][^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] After the Empire lost the [First Balkan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Balkan_War) (1912–1913), it lost all its [Balkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_peninsula) territories except [East Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Thrace) (European Turkey). This resulted in around 400,000 Muslims fleeing with the retreating Ottoman armies (with many dying from [cholera](7591.md) brought by the soldiers), and 400,000 non-Muslims fled territory still under Ottoman rule.[^cite_note-165] [Justin McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_McCarthy_\(American_historian\)) estimates that from 1821 to 1922, 5.5 million Muslims died in southeastern Europe, with the expulsion of 5 million.[^cite_note-McCarthy1995-166][^cite_note-Carmichael2012-167] In addition the Ottoman state encouraged transnational migration, including Circassians, Bosnians, and Russian Jews resettled in the [southern Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Levant). These communities, alongside internal Arab resettlement, reshaped rural society and land use in ways that reflected broader imperial patterns of demographic engineering and agrarian expansion.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-Buturovic2010-169]

### Defeat and dissolution (1908–1922)

Main articles: [Dissolution of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) and [Ottoman Empire in World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire_in_World_War_I)

#### Young Turk movement



Declaration of the [Young Turk Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turk_Revolution) by the leaders of the Ottoman [millets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\)) in 1908

The defeat and [dissolution of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) (1908–1922) began with the [Second Constitutional Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Constitutional_Era), a moment of hope and promise established with the [Young Turk Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turk_Revolution). It restored the [Constitution of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) and brought in [multi-party politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) with a [two-stage electoral system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) ([electoral law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_electoral_law)) under the [Ottoman parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Assembly_of_the_Ottoman_Empire). The constitution offered hope by freeing the empire's citizens to modernise the state's institutions, rejuvenate its strength, and enable it to hold its own against outside powers. Its guarantee of liberties promised to dissolve inter-communal tensions and transform the empire into a more harmonious place.[^cite_note-170] Instead, this period became the story of the twilight struggle of the Empire.

Members of [Young Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turks) movement who had once gone underground now established their parties.[^cite_note-171] Among them the [Committee of Union and Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Union_and_Progress) and the [Freedom and Accord Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_and_Accord_Party) were major parties. On the other end of the spectrum were ethnic parties, which included [Poale Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Social_Democratic_Labour_Party_in_Palestine_\(Poale_Zion\)), [Al-Fatat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Fatat), and [Armenian national movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_national_movement) organised under [Armenian Revolutionary Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Revolutionary_Federation). Profiting from the civil strife, Austria-Hungary officially annexed [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md) in 1908. The last of the [Ottoman censuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Census_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) was performed in [1914](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1914_population_statistics_for_the_Ottoman_Empire). Despite [military reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_military_reforms) which reconstituted the [Ottoman Modern Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Modern_Army), the Empire lost its North African territories and the Dodecanese in the [Italo-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italo-Turkish_War) (1911) and almost all of its European territories in the [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars) (1912–1913). The Empire faced continuous unrest in the years leading up to [World War I](4764461.md), including the [31 March Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/31_March_Incident) and two further coups in [1912](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1912_Ottoman_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and [1913](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1913_Ottoman_coup_d'%C3%A9tat).

#### World War I

Main articles: [Ottoman entry into World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_entry_into_World_War_I) and [Ottoman Empire in World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire_in_World_War_I)



Admiral [Wilhelm Souchon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Souchon), who commanded the [Black Sea raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_raid) on 29 October 1914, and his officers in Ottoman naval uniforms

The Ottoman Empire entered [World War I](4764461.md) on the side of the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) and was ultimately defeated.[^cite_note-172] The Ottoman participation in the war began with the combined [German-Ottoman surprise attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Raid) on the [Black Sea](3386.md) coast of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) on 29 October 1914. Following the attack, the Russian Empire (2 November 1914)[^cite_note-oxfordreference-timeline-173] and its allies [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Third_Republic) (5 November 1914)[^cite_note-oxfordreference-timeline-173] and the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) (5 November 1914)[^cite_note-oxfordreference-timeline-173] declared war on the Ottoman Empire. Also on 5 November 1914, the British government changed the status of the [Khedivate of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khedivate_of_Egypt) and [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprus_Convention), which were [de jure](43594.md) Ottoman territories prior to the war, to [British protectorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_protectorate).

The Ottomans successfully defended the [Dardanelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dardanelles) strait during the [Gallipoli campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallipoli_campaign) (1915–1916) and achieved initial victories against British forces in the first two years of the [Mesopotamian campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamian_campaign), such as the [Siege of Kut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Kut) (1915–1916); but the [Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt) (1916–1918) turned the tide against the Ottomans in the Middle East. In the [Caucasus campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_campaign), however, the Russian forces had the upper hand from the beginning, especially after the [Battle of Sarikamish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sarikamish) (1914–1915). Russian forces advanced into northeastern [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) and controlled the major cities there until retreating from World War I with the [Treaty of Brest-Litovsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Brest-Litovsk) following the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) in 1917.

#### Genocides

Main articles: [Late Ottoman genocides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Ottoman_genocides), [Armenian genocide](140376.md), [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide), and [Seyfo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seyfo)



The [Armenian genocide](140376.md) was the result of the Ottoman government's [deportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporary_Law_of_Deportation) and [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) policies regarding its [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) citizens after the [Battle of Sarikamish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sarikamish) (1914–1915) and the collapse of the [Caucasus Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_campaign) against the [Imperial Russian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Russian_Army) and [Armenian volunteer units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_volunteer_units) during [World War I](4764461.md). An estimated 600,000

[^cite_note-britannica-ag-174]

&#x20;to more than 1 million,

[^cite_note-britannica-ag-174]

&#x20;or up to 1.5 million

[^cite_note-175]

[^cite_note-176]

[^cite_note-177]

&#x20;people were killed.

In 1915 the Ottoman government and Kurdish tribes in the region started the extermination of its ethnic Armenian population, resulting in the deaths of up to 1.5 million Armenians in the [Armenian genocide](140376.md).[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180] The genocide was carried out during and after World War I and implemented in two phases: the wholesale killing of the able-bodied male population through massacre and subjection of army conscripts to forced labour, followed by the deportation of women, children, the elderly and infirm on [death marches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_march) leading to the [Syrian desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_desert). Driven forward by military escorts, the deportees were deprived of food and water and subjected to periodic robbery, [rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Armenian_Genocide), and systematic massacre.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] Large-scale massacres were also committed against the Empire's [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide) and [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_genocide) minorities as part of the same campaign of ethnic cleansing.[^cite_note-183]

#### Arab Revolt

Main articles: [Middle Eastern theatre of World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Eastern_theatre_of_World_War_I) and [Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt)

The [Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt) began in 1916 with British support. It turned the tide against the Ottomans on the Middle Eastern front, where they seemed to have the upper hand during the first two years of the war. On the basis of the [McMahon–Hussein Correspondence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McMahon%E2%80%93Hussein_Correspondence), an agreement between the British government and [Hussein bin Ali, Sharif of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_bin_Ali,_Sharif_of_Mecca), the revolt was officially initiated at Mecca on 10 June 1916.[^cite_note-185] The Arab nationalist goal was to create a single unified and independent [Arab state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_state) stretching from [Aleppo](159244.md), Syria, to [Aden](38050.md), Yemen, which the British promised to recognise.

The [Sharifian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharifian_Army), led by Hussein and the [Hashemites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashemites), with military backing from the British [Egyptian Expeditionary Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Expeditionary_Force), successfully fought and expelled the Ottoman military presence from much of the [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz) and [Transjordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transjordan_\(region\)). The rebellion eventually took [Damascus](8914.md) and set up a short-lived monarchy led by [Faisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_I_of_Iraq), a son of Hussein.

Following the terms of the 1916 [Sykes–Picot Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sykes%E2%80%93Picot_Agreement), the British and French later partitioned the Middle East into [mandate territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations_mandate). There was no unified Arab state, much to Arab nationalists' anger. Palestine, Iraq, Lebanon and Syria became British and French mandates.[^cite_note-186]

#### Treaty of Sèvres and Turkish War of Independence



[Mehmed VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_VI), the last Sultan of the Ottoman Empire, leaving the country after the abolition of the Ottoman sultanate, 17 November 1922

Defeated in World War I, the Ottoman Empire signed the [Armistice of Mudros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_of_Mudros) on 30 October 1918. [Istanbul was occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Istanbul) by combined British, French, Italian, and Greek forces. In May 1919, Greece also [took control of the area around Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Smyrna) (now İzmir).

The [partition of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) was finalised under the terms of the 1920 [Treaty of Sèvres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_S%C3%A8vres). This treaty, as designed in the [Conference of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conference_of_London_\(1920\)), allowed the Sultan to retain his position and title. Anatolia's status was problematic given the occupied forces.

A nationalist opposition arose in the [Turkish national movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_national_movement). It won the [Turkish War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_War_of_Independence) (1919–1923) under the leadership of [Mustafa Kemal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk) (later given the surname "Atatürk"). The sultanate was abolished on 1 November 1922, and the last sultan, [Mehmed VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_VI) (reigned 1918–1922), left the country on 17 November 1922. The [Republic of Turkey](11125639.md) was [established](28451535.md#Republic_of_Turkey) in its place on 29 October 1923, in the new capital city of [Ankara](802.md). The [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate) was abolished on 3 March 1924.[^cite_note-Ozoglu-187][^cite_note-188]

Several historians, such as British historian [Edward Gibbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Gibbon) and the Greek historian [Dimitri Kitsikis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitri_Kitsikis), have argued that after the fall of Constantinople, the Ottoman state took over the machinery of the Byzantine (Roman) state and that the Ottoman Empire was in essence a continuation of the Byzantine Empire under a [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people) [Muslim](19541.md) guise.[^cite_note-189] The American historian [Speros Vryonis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speros_Vryonis) writes that the Ottoman state centred on "a Byzantine-Balkan base with a veneer of the Turkish language and the Islamic religion".[^cite_note-Stone,_pp._86-100-190] Kitsikis and the American historian [Heath Lowry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heath_Lowry) posit that the early Ottoman state was a predatory confederacy open to both Byzantine Christians and Turkish Muslims whose primary goal was attaining booty and slaves, rather than spreading Islam, and that Islam only later became the empire's primary characteristic.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] Other historians have followed the lead of the Austrian historian [Paul Wittek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Wittek), who emphasises the early Ottoman state's Islamic character, seeing it as a "[jihad](16203.md) state" dedicated to expanding the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world).[^cite_note-Stone,_pp._86-100-190] Many historians led in 1937 by the Turkish historian [Mehmet Fuat Köprülü](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmet_Fuat_K%C3%B6pr%C3%BCl%C3%BC) championed the [Ghaza thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaza_thesis), according to which the early Ottoman state was a continuation of the way of life of the nomadic [Turkic tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) who had come from East Asia to Anatolia via Central Asia and the Middle East on a much larger scale. They argued that the most important cultural influences on the Ottoman state came from [Persia](14653.md).[^cite_note-194]

The British historian [Norman Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Stone) suggests many continuities between the Byzantine and Ottoman empires, such as that the *zeugarion* tax of Byzantium became the Ottoman *[Resm-i çift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resm-i_%C3%A7ift)* tax, that the *[pronoia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pronoia)* land-holding system that linked the amount of land one owned with one's ability to raise cavalry became the Ottoman *[timar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timar)* system, and that the Ottoman land measurement the *[dönüm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%B6n%C3%BCm)* was the same as the Byzantine *[stremma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stremma)*.
