Stone also argues that although Sunni Islam was the state religion, the Ottoman state supported and controlled the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md), which in return for accepting that control became the Ottoman Empire's largest land-holder. Despite the similarities, Stone argues that a crucial difference is that the land grants under the *timar* system were not hereditary at first. Even after they became inheritable, land ownership in the Ottoman Empire remained highly insecure, and the sultan revoked land grants whenever he wished. Stone argued this insecurity in land tenure strongly discouraged *[Timariots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timariot)* from seeking long-term development of their land, and instead led them to adopt a strategy of short-term exploitation, which had deleterious effects on the Ottoman economy.[^cite_note-195]

## Government

Main article: [State organisation of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_organisation_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)





[Topkapı Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkap%C4%B1_Palace) and [Dolmabahçe Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmabah%C3%A7e_Palace) were the primary residences of the [Ottoman sultans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sultans_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) in [Istanbul](3391396.md) between 1465 and 1856

[^cite_note-nytimes-196]

&#x20;and 1856 to 1922,

[^cite_note-dolmabahcepalace-197]

&#x20;respectively.

Before the reforms of the 19th and 20th centuries, the [state organisation of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_organisation_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) was a system with two main dimensions, the military administration, and the civil administration. The Sultan was in the highest position in the system. The civil system was based on local administrative units based on the region's characteristics. The state had control over the clergy. Certain pre-Islamic Turkish traditions that had survived the adoption of administrative and legal practices from Islamic [Iran](14653.md) remained important in Ottoman administrative circles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEItzkowitz198038-198] According to Ottoman understanding, the state's primary responsibility was to defend and extend the land of the Muslims and to ensure security and harmony within its borders in the overarching context of [orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) Islamic practice and dynastic sovereignty.[^cite_note-Kapucu-2008-199]

The Ottoman Empire, or as a dynastic institution, the House of Osman, was unprecedented and unequalled in the Islamic world for its size and duration.[^cite_note-200] In Europe, only the [House of Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg) had a similarly unbroken line of sovereigns (kings/emperors) from the same family who ruled for so long, and during the same period, between the late 13th and early 20th centuries. The Ottoman dynasty was Turkish in origin. On eleven occasions, the sultan was deposed (replaced by another sultan of the Ottoman dynasty, who were either the former sultan's brother, son or nephew) because he was perceived by his enemies as a threat to the state. There were only two attempts in Ottoman history to unseat the ruling Ottoman dynasty, both failures, which suggests a political system that for an extended period was able to manage its revolutions without unnecessary instability.[^cite_note-Kapucu-2008-199] As such, the last Ottoman sultan Mehmed VI (r. 1918–1922) was a [direct patrilineal (male-line) descendant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sultans_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) of the first Ottoman sultan [Osman I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_I) (d. 1323/4), which was unparalleled in both Europe (e.g., the male line of the House of Habsburg became extinct in 1740) and in the Islamic world. The primary purpose of the [Imperial Harem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Harem) was to ensure the birth of male heirs to the Ottoman throne and secure the continuation of the direct patrilineal (male-line) power of the Ottoman sultans in the future generations.



Ambassadors at the Topkapı Palace

The highest position in Islam, [caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), was claimed by the sultans starting with [Selim I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I),[^cite_note-Lambton-1995-26] which was established as the Ottoman Caliphate. The Ottoman sultan, *[pâdişâh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padishah)* or "lord of kings", served as the Empire's sole regent and was considered to be the embodiment of its government, though he did not always exercise complete control. The Imperial Harem was one of the most important powers of the Ottoman court. It was ruled by the [valide sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valide_sultan). On occasion, the valide sultan became involved in state politics. For a time, the women of the Harem effectively controlled the state in what was termed the "[Sultanate of Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_the_women)". New sultans were always chosen from the sons of the previous sultan. The strong educational system of the [palace school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_school) was geared towards eliminating the unfit potential heirs and establishing support among the ruling elite for a successor. The palace schools, which also educated the future administrators of the state, were not a single track. First, the [Madrasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa) (*Medrese*) was designated for the Muslims, and educated scholars and state officials according to Islamic tradition. The financial burden of the Medrese was supported by *[vakif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf)*&#x73;, allowing children of poor families to move to higher social levels and income.[^cite_note-201] The second track was a free [boarding school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boarding_school) for the Christians, the *[Enderûn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ender%C3%BBn)*,[^cite_note-202] which recruited 3,000 students annually from Christian boys between eight and twenty years old from one in forty families among the communities settled in [Rumelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumelia) or the Balkans, a process known as [Devshirme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devshirme_in_the_Ottoman_Palace_School) (*Devşirme*).[^cite_note-203] The Devshirme falls within modern definitions of [genocide](12441.md).[^cite_note-a852-204][^cite_note-v450-205]

Though the sultan was the supreme monarch, the sultan's political and executive authority was delegated. The politics of the state had a number of advisors and ministers gathered around a council known as [Divan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divan). The Divan, in the years when the Ottoman state was still a *[Beylik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bey)*, was composed of the elders of the tribe. Its composition was later modified to include military officers and local elites (such as religious and political advisors). Later still, beginning in 1320, a Grand Vizier was appointed to assume certain of the sultan's responsibilities. The Grand Vizier had considerable independence from the sultan with almost unlimited powers of appointment, dismissal, and supervision. Beginning with the late 16th century, sultans withdrew from politics and the Grand Vizier became the *de facto* head of state.[^cite_note-Black-2001-206]



[Yusuf Ziya Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_Ziya_Pasha), Ottoman ambassador to the United States, in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), 1913

Throughout Ottoman history, there were many instances in which local governors acted independently, and even in opposition to the ruler. After the Young Turk Revolution of 1908, the Ottoman state became a constitutional monarchy. The sultan no longer had executive powers. A parliament was formed, with representatives chosen from the provinces. The representatives formed the [Imperial Government of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Government_of_the_Ottoman_Empire).

This eclectic administration was apparent even in the diplomatic correspondence of the Empire, which was initially undertaken in the [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) to the west.[^cite_note-207]

The [Tughra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tughra) were calligraphic monograms, or signatures, of the Ottoman Sultans, of which there were 35. Carved on the Sultan's seal, they bore the names of the Sultan and his father. The statement and prayer, "ever victorious", was also present in most. The earliest belonged to Orhan Gazi. The ornately stylised *Tughra* spawned a branch of Ottoman-Turkish [calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calligraphy).

### Law

Main article: [Law of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



An unhappy wife complaining to the [Qadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadi) about her husband's [impotence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erectile_dysfunction), as depicted in an Ottoman miniature. [Divorce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divorce) is [allowed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_and_divorce#Islam) in [Islamic law](28840.md) and [can be initiated by either the husband or the wife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divorce_in_Islam).

[^cite_note-208]

The Ottoman legal system accepted the [religious law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_law) over its subjects. At the same time the *[Qanun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanun_\(law\))* (or *Kanun*), dynastic law, co-existed with religious law or [Sharia](28840.md).[^cite_note-otmkanun-209][^cite_note-210] The Ottoman Empire was always organised around a system of local [jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisprudence). Legal administration in the Ottoman Empire was part of a larger scheme of balancing central and local authority.[^cite_note-Benton-2001-211] Ottoman power revolved crucially around the administration of the rights to land, which gave a space for the local authority to develop the needs of the local [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\)).[^cite_note-Benton-2001-211] The jurisdictional complexity of the Ottoman Empire was aimed to permit the integration of culturally and religiously different groups.[^cite_note-Benton-2001-211] The Ottoman system had three court systems: one for Muslims, one for non-Muslims, involving appointed Jews and Christians ruling over their respective religious communities, and the "trade court". The entire system was regulated from above by means of the administrative *Qanun*, i.e., laws, a system based upon the Turkic *[Yassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yassa)* and *[Töre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%B6re_\(law\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)*, which were developed in the pre-Islamic era.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213]

These court categories were not, however, wholly exclusive; for instance, the Islamic courts, which were the Empire's primary courts, could also be used to settle a trade conflict or disputes between litigants of differing religions, and Jews and Christians often went to them to obtain a more forceful ruling on an issue. The Ottoman state tended not to interfere with non-Muslim religious law systems, despite legally having a voice to do so through local governors. The Islamic *Sharia* law system had been developed from a combination of the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an); the [Hadīth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith), or words of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam); *[ijmā'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma)*, or consensus of the members of the [Muslim community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah); [qiyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyas), a system of analogical reasoning from earlier precedents; and local customs. Both systems were taught at the Empire's law schools, which were in [Istanbul](3391396.md) and [Bursa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bursa).

The Ottoman Islamic legal system was set up differently from traditional European courts. Presiding over Islamic courts was a *Qadi*, or judge. Since the closing of the *[ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)*, or 'Gate of Interpretation', *Qadi*s throughout the Ottoman Empire focused less on legal precedent, and more with local customs and traditions in the areas that they administered.[^cite_note-Benton-2001-211] However, the Ottoman court system lacked an appellate structure, leading to jurisdictional case strategies where plaintiffs could take their disputes from one court system to another until they achieved a ruling that was in their favour.



An Ottoman trial, 1877

In the late 19th century, the Ottoman legal system saw substantial reform. This process of legal modernisation began with the [Edict of Gülhane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_G%C3%BClhane) of 1839.[^cite_note-review-niza-214] These reforms included the "fair and public trial\[s] of all accused regardless of religion", the creation of a system of "separate competences, religious and civil", and the validation of testimony on non-Muslims.[^cite_note-int-handbook-215] Specific land codes (1858), civil codes (1869–1876), and a code of civil procedure also were enacted.[^cite_note-int-handbook-215]

These reforms were based heavily on French models, as indicated by the adoption of a three-tiered court system. Referred to as [Nizamiye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizamiye), this system was extended to the local magistrate level with the final promulgation of the [Mecelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecelle), a civil code that regulated marriage, divorce, alimony, will, and other matters of personal status.[^cite_note-int-handbook-215] In an attempt to clarify the division of judicial competences, an administrative council laid down that religious matters were to be handled by religious courts, and statute matters were to be handled by the Nizamiye courts.[^cite_note-int-handbook-215]

### Military

Main article: [Military of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



Ottoman [sipahis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sipahi) in battle, holding the crescent banner, by [Józef Brandt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Brandt)

The first military unit of the Ottoman State was an army that was organised by Osman I from the tribesmen inhabiting the hills of western Anatolia in the late 13th century. The military system became an intricate organisation with the advance of the Empire. The Ottoman military was a complex system of recruiting and fief-holding. The main corps of the [Ottoman Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Army) included Janissary, [Sipahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sipahi), [Akıncı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akinji) and [Mehterân](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_military_band). The Ottoman army was once among the most advanced fighting forces in the world, being one of the first to use muskets and cannons. The Ottoman Turks began using *[falconets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falconet_\(cannon\))*, which were short but wide cannons, during the [Siege of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_\(1422\)). The Ottoman cavalry depended on high speed and mobility rather than heavy armour, using bows and short swords on fast [Turkoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkoman_horse) and [Arabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_horse) horses (progenitors of the [Thoroughbred](18842022.md#Foundation_stallions) racing horse),[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217] and often applied tactics similar to those of the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire), such as pretending to retreat while surrounding the enemy forces inside a crescent-shaped formation and then making the real attack. The Ottoman army continued to be an effective fighting force throughout the seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries,[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] falling behind the empire's European rivals only during a long period of peace from 1740 to 1768.[^cite_note-AksanOW-79]



Modernised [Ertugrul Cavalry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asakir-i_Mansure-i_Muhammediye) crossing the [Galata Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata_Bridge) in 1901

The modernisation of the Ottoman Empire in the 19th century started with the military. In 1826 Sultan Mahmud II abolished the Janissary corps and established the modern Ottoman army. He named them as the [Nizam-ı Cedid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizam-%C4%B1_Cedid) (New Order). The Ottoman army was also the first institution to hire foreign experts and send its officers for training in western European countries. Consequently, the Young Turks movement began when these relatively young and newly trained men returned with their education.



The [Ottoman fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Navy) in the [Bosphorous](3705.md) near [Ortaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ortak%C3%B6y)

The [Ottoman Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Navy) vastly contributed to the expansion of the Empire's territories on the European continent. It initiated the conquest of North Africa, with the addition of [Algeria](358.md) and Egypt to the Ottoman Empire in 1517. Starting with the loss of Greece in 1821 and Algeria in 1830, Ottoman naval power and control over the Empire's distant overseas territories began to decline. Sultan [Abdülaziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd%C3%BClaziz) (reigned 1861–1876) attempted to reestablish a strong Ottoman navy, building the largest fleet after those of Britain and France. The shipyard at Barrow, England, built its first [submarine](28825.md) in 1886 for the Ottoman Empire.[^cite_note-first_submarine_at_shipyard-220]

However, the collapsing Ottoman economy could not sustain the fleet's strength for long. Sultan [Abdülhamid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd%C3%BClhamid_II) distrusted the admirals who sided with the reformist [Midhat Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midhat_Pasha) and claimed that the large and expensive fleet was of no use against the Russians during the Russo-Turkish War. He locked most of the fleet inside the [Golden Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horn), where the ships decayed for the next 30 years. Following the Young Turk Revolution in 1908, the Committee of Union and Progress sought to develop a strong Ottoman naval force. The *Ottoman Navy Foundation* was established in 1910 to buy new ships through public donations.



[Ottoman pilots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Aviation_Squadrons) in early 1912

The establishment of [Ottoman military aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Air_Force) dates back to between June 1909 and July 1911.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] The Ottoman Empire started preparing its first pilots and planes, and with the founding of the Aviation School (*Tayyare Mektebi*) in [Yeşilköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ye%C5%9Filk%C3%B6y) on 3 July 1912, the Empire began to tutor its own flight officers. The founding of the Aviation School quickened advancement in the military aviation programme, increased the number of enlisted persons within it, and gave the new pilots an active role in the Ottoman Army and Navy. In May 1913, the world's first specialised Reconnaissance Training Program was started by the Aviation School, and the first separate reconnaissance division was established. In June 1914 a new military academy, the Naval Aviation School (*Bahriye Tayyare Mektebi*) was founded. With the outbreak of World War I, the modernisation process stopped abruptly. The [Ottoman Aviation Squadrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Aviation_Squadrons) fought on many fronts during World War I, from [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Central_Europe\)) in the west to the Caucasus in the east and [Yemen](350939.md) in the south.

## Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



Administrative divisions of the Ottoman Empire in 1899 (year 1317 Hijri)

The Ottoman Empire was first subdivided into provinces, in the sense of fixed territorial units with governors appointed by the sultan, in the late 14th century.[^cite_note-Imber-223]

The [eyalet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyalet) (also *pashalik* or *beylerbeylik*) was the territory of office of a [beylerbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beylerbey) ("lord of lords" or governor), and was further subdivided into [Sanjaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjak).[^cite_note-224]

The [vilayets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilayets) were introduced with the promulgation of the "Vilayet Law" (*Teskil-i Vilayet Nizamnamesi*)[^cite_note-225] in 1864, as part of the Tanzimat reforms.[^cite_note-trt-226] Unlike the previous eyalet system, the 1864 law established a hierarchy of administrative units: the vilayet, [liva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liva_\(sanjak\))/[sanjak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjak)/[mutasarrifate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutasarrifate), [kaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaza) and [village council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_council), to which the 1871 Vilayet Law added the [nahiye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahiye).[^cite_note-jpn-227]

## Economy

Main article: [Economic history of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



Coins of the Sultanate of Rûm and the Ottoman Empire at [Aydın Archaeological Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayd%C4%B1n_Archaeological_Museum)

The Ottoman government deliberately pursued a policy for the development of Bursa, Edirne, and Istanbul, successive Ottoman capitals, into major commercial and industrial centres, considering that merchants and artisans were indispensable in creating a new metropolis.[^cite_note-Inalcik1970209-228] To this end, Mehmed and his successor Bayezid, also encouraged and welcomed migration of the Jews from different parts of Europe, who were settled in Istanbul and other port cities like Salonica. In many places in Europe, Jews were suffering persecution at the hands of their Christian counterparts, such as in Spain, after the conclusion of the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista). The tolerance displayed by the Turks was welcomed by the immigrants.



A European bronze medal from the period of [Sultan Mehmed the Conqueror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II), 1481

The Ottoman economic mind was closely related to the basic concepts of state and society in the Middle East in which the ultimate goal of a state was consolidation and extension of the ruler's power, and the way to reach it was to get rich resources of revenues by making the productive classes prosperous.[^cite_note-Inalcik1970217-229] The ultimate aim was to increase the state revenues without damaging the prosperity of subjects to prevent the emergence of social disorder and to keep the traditional organisation of the society intact. The Ottoman economy greatly expanded during the early modern period, with particularly high growth rates during the first half of the 18th century. The empire's annual income quadrupled between 1523 and 1748, adjusted for inflation.[^cite_note-230]

The organisation of the treasury and chancery were developed under the Ottoman Empire more than any other Islamic government and, until the 17th century, they were the leading organisation among all their contemporaries.[^cite_note-Black-2001-206] This organisation developed a scribal bureaucracy (known as "men of the pen") as a distinct group, partly highly trained ulama, which developed into a professional body.[^cite_note-Black-2001-206] The effectiveness of this professional financial body stands behind the success of many great Ottoman statesmen.[^cite_note-231]



The [Ottoman Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Bank) was founded in 1856 in Constantinople. On 26 August 1896, the bank was [occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Ottoman_Bank) by members of the [Armenian Revolutionary Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Revolutionary_Federation).

Modern Ottoman studies indicate that the change in relations between the Ottoman Turks and central Europe was caused by the opening of the new sea routes. It is possible to see the decline in the significance of the land routes to the East as Western Europe opened the ocean routes that bypassed the Middle East and the Mediterranean as parallel to the decline of the Ottoman Empire itself.[^cite_note-232] The [Anglo-Ottoman Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Ottoman_Treaty), also known as the [Treaty of Balta Liman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Balta_Liman) that opened the Ottoman markets directly to English and French competitors, can be seen as one of the staging posts along with this development.

By developing commercial centres and routes, encouraging people to extend the area of cultivated land in the country and international trade through its dominions, the state performed basic economic functions in the Empire. But in all this, the financial and political interests of the state were dominant. Within the social and political system they were living in, Ottoman administrators could not see the desirability of the dynamics and principles of the capitalist and mercantile economies developing in Western Europe.[^cite_note-Inalcik1970218-233]

Economic historian [Paul Bairoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Bairoch) argues that [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) contributed to [deindustrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deindustrialisation) in the Ottoman Empire. In contrast to the [protectionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) of China, Japan, and Spain, the Ottoman Empire had a [liberal trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism) policy, open to foreign imports. This has origins in [capitulations of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitulations_of_the_Ottoman_Empire), dating back to the first commercial treaties signed with France in 1536 and taken further with [capitulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitulation_\(treaty\)) in 1673 and 1740, which lowered [duties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duty_\(economics\)) to 3% for imports and exports. The liberal Ottoman policies were praised by British economists, such as [John Ramsay McCulloch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ramsay_McCulloch) in his *Dictionary of Commerce* (1834), but later criticised by British politicians such as Prime Minister Benjamin Disraeli, who cited the Ottoman Empire as "an instance of the injury done by unrestrained competition" in the 1846 [Corn Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Laws) debate.[^cite_note-234]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



[İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir) under Ottoman rule in 1900

A population estimate for the empire of 11,692,480 for the 1520–1535 period was obtained by counting the households in Ottoman tithe registers, and multiplying this number by 5.[^cite_note-Kabadayı-235] For unclear reasons, the population in the 18th century was lower than that in the 16th century.[^cite_note-236] An estimate of 7,230,660 for the first census held in 1831 is considered a serious undercount, as this census was meant only to register possible conscripts.[^cite_note-Kabadayı-235]

Censuses of Ottoman territories only began in the early 19th century. Figures from 1831 onwards are available as official census results, but the censuses did not cover the whole population. For example, the 1831 census only counted men and did not cover the whole empire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinross1979281-111][^cite_note-Kabadayı-235] For earlier periods estimates of size and distribution of the population are based on observed demographic patterns.[^cite_note-237]



View of [Galata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata) ([Karaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karak%C3%B6y)) and the [Galata Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata_Bridge) on the [Golden Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horn), c. 1880–1893

However, it began to rise to reach 25–32 million by 1800, with around 10 million in the European provinces (primarily in the Balkans), 11 million in the Asiatic provinces, and around 3 million in the African provinces. Population densities were higher in the European provinces, double those in Anatolia, which in turn were triple the population densities of Iraq and [Syria](7515849.md) and five times the population density of Arabia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataertSpivey2000110–111-238]

Towards the end of the empire's existence life expectancy was 49 years, compared to the mid-twenties in Serbia at the beginning of the 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataertSpivey2000112-239] Epidemic diseases and famine caused major disruption and demographic changes. In 1785 around one-sixth of the Egyptian population died from the plague and Aleppo saw its population reduced by twenty percent in the 18th century. Six famines hit Egypt alone between 1687 and 1731 and the last famine to hit Anatolia was four decades later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataertSpivey2000113-240] The [1812–1819 Ottoman plague epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1812%E2%80%931819_Ottoman_plague_epidemic) would cost the lives of at least 300,000 individuals.[^cite_note-241]

The rise of port cities saw the clustering of populations caused by the development of steamships and railroads. Urbanisation increased from 1700 to 1922, with towns and cities growing. Improvements in health and sanitation made them more attractive to live and work in. Port cities like Salonica, in Greece, saw its population rise from 55,000 in 1800 to 160,000 in 1912 and İzmir which had a population of 150,000 in 1800 grew to 300,000 by 1914.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataertSpivey2000114-242][^cite_note-243] Some regions conversely had population falls—Belgrade saw its population drop from 25,000 to 8,000 mainly due to political strife.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataertSpivey2000114-242]



The town of [Safranbolu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safranbolu) is one of the best-preserved Ottoman villages.

Economic and political migrations made an impact across the empire. For example, the [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and Austria-Habsburg annexation of the Crimean and Balkan regions respectively saw large influxes of Muslim refugees—200,000 Crimean Tatars fleeing to Dobruja.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataertSpivey2000115-244] Between 1783 and 1913, approximately 5–7 million refugees arrived into the Ottoman Empire. Between the 1850s and World War I, about a million North Caucasian Muslims arrived in the Ottoman Empire as refugees.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamed-Troyansky202449-151] Some migrations left indelible marks such as political tension between parts of the empire (e.g., Turkey and Bulgaria), whereas centrifugal effects were noticed in other territories, simpler demographics emerging from diverse populations. Economies were also impacted by the loss of artisans, merchants, manufacturers, and agriculturists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataertSpivey2000116-245] Since the 19th century, a large proportion of Muslim peoples from the Balkans emigrated to present-day Turkey. These people are called *[Muhacir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhacir)*.[^cite_note-246] By the time the Ottoman Empire came to an end in 1922, half of the urban population of Turkey was descended from Muslim refugees from Russia.[^cite_note-books.google_b-143]

### Language

Main article: [Languages of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



1911 Ottoman calendar shown in several different languages such as: Ottoman Turkish, Greek, Armenian, Hebrew, Bulgarian and French

[Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish) was the official language of the Empire.[^cite_note-247] It was an [Oghuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_languages) [Turkic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages) highly influenced by [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language) and [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic), though lower registries spoken by the common people had fewer influences from other languages compared to higher varieties used by upper classes and governmental authorities.[^cite_note-248] Turkish, in its Ottoman variation, was a language of military and administration since the nascent days of the Ottomans. The Ottoman constitution of 1876 did officially cement the official imperial status of Turkish.[^cite_note-249]

The Ottomans had several influential languages: Turkish, spoken by the majority of the people in Anatolia and by the majority of Muslims of the Balkans except some regions such as [Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Albania), [Bosnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Bosnia)[^cite_note-Bertold_Spuler_page_69-250] and the [Megleno-Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megleno-Romanians)-inhabited [Nânti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%A2nti);[^cite_note-251] Persian, only spoken by the educated;[^cite_note-Bertold_Spuler_page_69-250] Arabic, spoken mainly in Egypt, the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Arabia), Iraq, North Africa, [Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Kuwait) and parts of the [Horn of Africa](62142.md) and [Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_language) in North Africa. In the last two centuries, usage of these became limited, though, and specific: Persian served mainly as a literary language for the educated,[^cite_note-Bertold_Spuler_page_69-250] while [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) was used for Islamic prayers. In the post-[Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat) period French became the common Western language among the educated.[^cite_note-Strauss-2010-24]

Because of a low literacy rate among the public (about 2–3% until the early 19th century and just about 15% at the end of the 19th century), ordinary people had to hire [scribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scribe) as "special request-writers" (*arzuhâlci*s) to be able to communicate with the government.[^cite_note-252] Some ethnic groups continued to speak within their families and neighbourhoods ([mahalles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahalle)) with their own languages, though many non-Muslim minorities such as Greeks and Armenians only spoke Turkish.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavison196462-253] In villages where two or more populations lived together, the inhabitants often spoke each other's language. In cosmopolitan cities, people often spoke their family languages; in some cities, many of those who were not ethnic [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people) spoke Turkish as a second language. However, not all cities used Turkish as a lingua franca.[^cite_note-254]

### Religion

See also: [Millet (Ottoman Empire)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\))



[Abdülmecid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulmejid_II) was the last [caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) of Islam and a member of the [Ottoman dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_dynasty).

[Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) was the prevailing *[Dīn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Din_\(Arabic\))* (customs, legal traditions, and religion) of the Ottoman Empire; the official *[Madh'hab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhhab)* (school of Islamic [jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisprudence)) was *[Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi).*[^cite_note-Gunduz-255] From the early 16th century until the early 20th century, the Ottoman sultan also served as the [caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), or politico-religious leader, of the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world). Most of the Ottoman Sultans adhered to [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) and followed [Sufi orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi_orders), and believed Sufism was the correct way to reach God.[^cite_note-256]

Non-Muslims, particularly Christians and Jews, were present throughout the empire's history. The Ottoman imperial system was charactised by an intricate combination of official Muslim hegemony over non-Muslims and a wide degree of religious tolerance. While religious minorities were never equal under the law, they were granted recognition, protection, and limited freedoms under both Islamic and Ottoman tradition.[^cite_note-emigrnonm-257]

Until the second half of the 15th century, the majority of Ottoman subjects were Christian.[^cite_note-Benton-2001-211] Non-Muslims remained a significant and economically influential minority, albeit declining significantly by the 19th century, due largely to migration and [secession](144732.md).[^cite_note-emigrnonm-257] The proportion of Muslims amounted to 60% in the 1820s, gradually increasing to 69% in the 1870s and 76% in the 1890s.[^cite_note-emigrnonm-257] By 1914, less than a fifth of the empire's population (19.1%) was non-Muslim, mostly made up of Jews and Christian Greeks, Assyrians, and Armenians.[^cite_note-emigrnonm-257]

#### Islam

Main articles: [Islam in the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_Ottoman_Empire), [Ottoman Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate), and [Ottoman persecution of Alevis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_persecution_of_Alevis)

See also: [Islam in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Turkey)

[Turkic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) practiced a form of [shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamanism) before adopting Islam. The [Muslim conquest of Transoxiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Transoxiana) under the [Abbasids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) facilitated the spread of Islam into the Turkic heartland of Central Asia. Many Turkic tribes—including the [Oghuz Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Turks), who were the ancestors of both the Seljuks and the Ottomans—gradually converted to Islam and brought religion to Anatolia through their migrations beginning in the 11th century. From its founding, the Ottoman Empire officially supported the [Maturidi school of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maturidi) [Islamic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Islamic_theology), which emphasised [human reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reason), [rationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationality), the pursuit of science and [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_philosophy) (*falsafa*).[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] The Ottomans were among the earliest and most enthusiastic adopters of the [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi) school of Islamic jurisprudence,[^cite_note-260] which was comparatively more flexible and discretionary in its rulings.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262]



The [Yıldız Hamidiye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C4%B1ld%C4%B1z_Hamidiye_Mosque) in [Istanbul](3391396.md), Turkey

The Ottoman Empire had a wide variety of Islamic sects, including [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze), [Ismailis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'ilism), [Alevis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alevism), and [Alawites](436616.md).[^cite_note-263] [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism), a diverse body of Islamic [mysticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysticism), found fertile ground in Ottoman lands; many Sufi religious orders (*[tariqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariqa)*), such as the [Bektashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bektashi_Order) and [Mevlevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mevlevi_Order), were either established, or saw significant growth, throughout the empire's history.[^cite_note-264] However, some heterodox Muslim groups were viewed as heretical and even ranked below Jews and Christians in terms of legal protection; Druze were frequent targets of persecution,[^cite_note-265] with Ottoman authorities often citing the controversial rulings of [Ibn Taymiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Taymiyyah), a member of the conservative [Hanbali school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali).[^cite_note-266] In 1514, Sultan Selim I ordered the massacre of 40,000 Anatolian Alevis (*[Qizilbash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qizilbash)*), whom he considered a [fifth column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_column) for the rival [Safavid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran).

During Selim's reign, the Ottoman Empire saw an unprecedented and rapid expansion into the Middle East, particularly the [conquest of the entire Mamluk Sultanate of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Mamluk_War_\(1516%E2%80%931517\)) on the early 16th century. These conquests further solidified the Ottoman claim of being an [Islamic caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), although Ottoman sultans had been claiming the title of caliph since the reign of Murad I (1362–1389).[^cite_note-Lambton-1995-26] The caliphate was officially transferred from the Mamluks to the Ottoman sultanate in 1517, whose members were recognised as caliphs until the [office's abolition on 3 March 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolition_of_the_Caliphate) by the [Republic of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey) (and the exile of the last caliph, [Abdülmecid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulmejid_II), to France).

#### Christianity and Judaism

Main articles: [Christianity in the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) and [History of the Jews in the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Ottoman_Empire)



[Mehmed the Conqueror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II) and Patriarch [Gennadius II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gennadius_Scholarius)

In accordance with the Muslim *[dhimmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi)* system, the Ottoman Empire guaranteed limited freedoms to Christians, Jews, and other "[people of the book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_of_the_Book)", such as the right to worship, own property, and be exempt from the obligatory alms (*[zakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat)*) required of Muslims. However, non-Muslims (or *dhimmi*) were subject to various legal restrictions, including being forbidden to carry weapons, ride on horseback, or have their homes overlook those of Muslims; likewise, they were required to pay higher taxes than Muslim subjects, including the *[jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya),* which was a key source of state revenue.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] Many Christians and Jews converted to Islam to secure full social and legal status, though most continued to practice their faith without restriction.

The Ottomans developed a unique sociopolitical system known as the [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\)), which granted non-Muslim communities a large degree of political, legal, and religious autonomy; in essence, members of a millet were subjects of the empire but not subject to the Muslim faith or Islamic law. A millet could govern its own affairs, such as raising taxes and resolving internal legal disputes, with little or no interference from Ottoman authorities, so long as its members were loyal to the sultan and adhered to the rules concerning *dhimmi.* A quintessential example is the ancient Orthodox community of [Mount Athos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Athos), which was permitted to retain its autonomy and was never subject to occupation or forced conversion; even special laws were enacted to protect it from outsiders.[^cite_note-269]

The [*Rum* Millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rum_Millet), which encompassed most Eastern Orthodox Christians, was governed by the Byzantine-era *[Corpus Juris Civilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpus_Juris_Civilis)* (Code of Justinian), with the [Ecumenical Patriarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople) designated the highest religious and political authority (*millet-bashi*, or [ethnarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnarch)). Likewise, [Ottoman Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) came under the authority of the *[Haham Başı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakham_Bashi),* or Ottoman [Chief Rabbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Rabbi), while [Armenians](387816.md) were under the authority of the [chief bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicos_of_All_Armenians) of the [Armenian Apostolic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church).[^cite_note-Syed-2011-270] As the largest group of non-Muslim subjects, the Rum Millet enjoyed several special privileges in politics and commerce; however, Jews and Armenians were also well represented among the wealthy merchant class, as well as in public administration.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272]

Some modern scholars consider the millet system to be an early example of [religious pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_pluralism), as it accorded minority religious groups official recognition and tolerance.[^cite_note-273]

### Social-political-religious structure

See also: [Social class in the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) and [Rayah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayah)



Ethnic map of the Ottoman Empire in 1917. Black = Bulgars and Turks, Red = Greeks, Light yellow = Armenians, Blue = Kurds, Orange = [Lazes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laz_people), Dark Yellow = Arabs, Green = [Nestorians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East)

Beginning in the early 19th century, society, government, and religion were interrelated in a complex, overlapping way that was deemed inefficient by Atatürk, who systematically dismantled it after 1922.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] In Constantinople, the Sultan ruled two distinct domains: the secular government and the religious hierarchy. Religious officials formed the Ulama, who had control of religious teachings and theology, and also the Empire's judicial system, giving them a major voice in day-to-day affairs in communities across the Empire (but not including the non-Muslim millets). They were powerful enough to reject the military reforms proposed by Sultan [Selim III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_III). His successor Sultan [Mahmud II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_II) (r. 1808–1839) first won ulama approval before proposing similar reforms.[^cite_note-276] The secularisation programme brought by Atatürk ended the ulema and their institutions. The caliphate was abolished, madrasas were closed down, and the sharia courts were abolished. He replaced the Arabic alphabet with Latin letters, ended the religious school system, and gave women some political rights. Many rural traditionalists never accepted this secularisation, and by the 1990s they were reasserting a demand for a larger role for Islam.[^cite_note-277]



The original [Church of St. Anthony of Padua, Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._Anthony_of_Padua,_Istanbul) was built in 1725 by the local Italian community of Istanbul.

The Janissaries were a highly formidable military unit in the early years, but as Western Europe modernised its military organisation technology, the Janissaries became a reactionary force that resisted all change. Steadily the Ottoman military power became outdated, but when the Janissaries felt their privileges were being threatened, or outsiders wanted to modernise them, or they might be superseded by the cavalrymen, they rose in rebellion. The rebellions were highly violent on both sides, but by the time the Janissaries were suppressed, it was far too late for Ottoman military power to catch up with the West.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] The political system was transformed by the destruction of the [Janissaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janissary), a powerful military/governmental/police force, which revolted in the [Auspicious Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auspicious_Incident) of 1826. Sultan Mahmud II crushed the revolt, executed the leaders and disbanded the large organisation. That set the stage for a slow process of modernisation of government functions, as the government sought, with mixed success, to adopt the main elements of Western bureaucracy and military technology.

The Janissaries had been recruited from Christians and other minorities; their abolition enabled the emergence of a Turkish elite to control the Ottoman Empire. A large number of ethnic and religious minorities were tolerated in their own separate segregated domains called millets.[^cite_note-280] They were primarily [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Greeks), [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_the_Ottoman_Empire), or [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Ottoman_Empire). In each locality, they governed themselves, spoke their own language, ran their own schools, cultural and religious institutions, and paid somewhat higher taxes. They had no power outside the millet. The Imperial government protected them and prevented major violent clashes between ethnic groups.

Ethnic nationalism, based on distinctive religion and language, provided a centripetal force that eventually destroyed the Ottoman Empire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuataert1983-281] In addition, Muslim ethnic groups, which were not part of the millet system, especially the Arabs and the Kurds, were outside the Turkish culture and developed their own separate nationalism. The British sponsored Arab nationalism in the First World War, promising an independent Arab state in return for Arab support. Most Arabs supported the Sultan, but those near Mecca believed in and supported the British promise.[^cite_note-282]



[Hemdat Israel Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemdat_Israel_Synagogue) of [Istanbul](3391396.md)

At the local level, power was held beyond the control of the Sultan by the [ayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayan_\(class\)) or local notables. The ayan collected taxes, formed local armies to compete with other notables, took a reactionary attitude toward political or economic change, and often defied policies handed down by the Sultan.[^cite_note-283]

After the 18th century, the Ottoman Empire was shrinking, as Russia put on heavy pressure and expanded to its south; Egypt became effectively independent in 1805, and the British later took it over, along with Cyprus. Greece became independent, and Serbia and other Balkan areas became highly restive as the force of nationalism pushed against imperialism. The French took over Algeria and Tunisia. The Europeans all thought that the empire was a sick man in rapid decline. Only the Germans seemed helpful, and their support led to the Ottoman Empire joining the central powers in 1915, with the result that they came out as one of the heaviest losers of the First World War in 1918.[^cite_note-284]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)



Depiction of a [hookah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hookah) shop in [Lebanon](17771.md)

The Ottomans absorbed some of the traditions, art, and institutions of cultures in the regions they conquered and added new dimensions to them. Numerous traditions and cultural traits of previous empires (in fields such as architecture, cuisine, music, leisure, and government) were adopted by the Ottoman Turks, who developed them into new forms, resulting in a new and distinctively Ottoman cultural identity. Although the predominant literary language of the Ottoman Empire was Turkish, Persian was the preferred vehicle for the projection of an imperial image.[^cite_note-285]

[Slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) was part of Ottoman society,[^cite_note-286] with most slaves employed as domestic servants or in the case of female slaves as [concubines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concubinage_in_Islam) ([sexual slaves](173634.md)). Agricultural slavery, such as that [in the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), was relatively rare. The child of a slave was born a slave, unless the male slave owner acknowledged the child of his female slave as his, in which case the child would be automatically free by law.[^cite_note-287] Female slaves were still sold in the Empire as late as 1908.[^cite_note-288] During the 19th century the Empire came under pressure from Western European countries to outlaw the practice. Policies developed by various sultans throughout the 19th century attempted to curtail the [Ottoman slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_slave_trade) but slavery had centuries of religious backing and sanction and so was never abolished in the Empire.[^cite_note-Syed-2011-270]

Ottomans adopted Persian bureaucratic traditions and culture. The sultans also made an important contribution in the development of [Persian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature).[^cite_note-289]

Language was not an obvious sign of group connection and identity in the 16th century among the rulers of the Ottoman Empire, [Safavid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran) and [Abu'l-Khayrid Shibanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaybanids) of [Central Asia](6742.md).[^cite_note-Comstock-Skipp-2023-290] Hence the ruling classes of all three polities were bilingual in varieties of Persian and Turkic.[^cite_note-Comstock-Skipp-2023-290] But in the century's final quarter, linguistic adjustments occurred in the Ottoman and Safavid realms defined by a new rigidity that favoured Ottoman Turkish and Persian, respectively.[^cite_note-Comstock-Skipp-2023-290]

### Education

Main article: [Education in the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_Ottoman_Empire)



The Beyazıt State Library was founded in 1884.

In the Ottoman Empire, each [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\)) established a schooling system serving its members.[^cite_note-Strauss-2016-291] Education was therefore largely divided on ethnic and religious lines: few non-Muslims attended schools for Muslim students, and vice versa. Most institutions that served all ethnic and religious groups taught in French or other languages.[^cite_note-StraussPT195-292]

Several "foreign schools" (*Frerler mektebleri*) operated by religious clergy primarily served Christians, although some Muslim students attended.[^cite_note-Strauss-2016-291] Garnett described the schools for Christians and Jews as "organised upon European models", with "voluntary contributions" supporting their operation and most of them "well attended" and with "a high standard of education".[^cite_note-293]

### Literature

Main article: [Ottoman literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_literature)

The two primary streams of Ottoman written literature are poetry and [prose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prose). Poetry was by far the dominant stream. The earliest work of Ottoman historiography for example, the [*İskendernâme*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iskendername), was composed by the poet [Taceddin Ahmedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taceddin_Ahmedi).[^cite_note-\:1-294] Until the 19th century, Ottoman prose did not contain any examples of fiction: there were no counterparts to, for instance, the European [romance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chivalric_romance), short story, or novel. Analog genres did exist, though, in both [Turkish folk literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_folk_literature) and in [Divan poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwan_\(poetry\)).

Ottoman Divan poetry was a highly ritualised and symbolic art form. From the [Persian poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature) that largely inspired it, it inherited a wealth of symbols whose meanings and interrelationships—both of similitude (مراعات نظير *mura'ât-i nazîr* / تناسب *tenâsüb*) and opposition (تضاد *tezâd*) were more or less prescribed. Divan poetry was composed through the constant juxtaposition of many such images within a strict metrical framework, allowing numerous potential meanings to emerge. The vast majority of Divan poetry was [lyric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyric_poetry) in nature: either [gazels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazel) (which make up the greatest part of the repertoire of the tradition), or kasîdes. But there were other common genres, especially the mesnevî, a kind of [verse romance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courtly_romance) and thus a variety of [narrative poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_poetry); the two most notable examples of this form are the [Leyli and Majnun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leyli_and_Majnun_\(Fuzuli\)) of [Fuzuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzuli_\(poet\)) and the [Hüsn ü Aşk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BCsn_%C3%BC_A%C5%9Fk) of [Şeyh Gâlib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eeyh_G%C3%A2lib). The [Seyahatnâme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seyahatn%C3%A2me) of [Evliya Çelebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evliya_%C3%87elebi) (1611–1682) is an outstanding example of travel literature.



[Ahmet Nedîm Efendi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ned%C3%AEm), one of the most celebrated Ottoman poets

Until the 19th century, [Ottoman prose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prose_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) did not develop to the extent that contemporary Divan poetry did. A large part of the reason was that much prose was expected to adhere to the rules of *sec* (سجع, also transliterated as *seci*), or [rhymed prose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhymed_prose),[^cite_note-295] a type of writing descended from the Arabic [saj'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saj') that prescribed that between each adjective and noun in a string of words, such as a sentence, there must be a rhyme. Nevertheless, there was a tradition of prose in the literature of the time, though it was exclusively nonfictional. One apparent exception was Muhayyelât (*Fancies*) by [Giritli Ali Aziz Efendi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giritli_Ali_Aziz_Efendi), a collection of stories of the fantastic written in 1796, though not published until 1867. The first novel published in the Ottoman Empire was [Vartan Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vartan_Pasha)'s 1851 *The Story of Akabi* (Turkish: *Akabi Hikyayesi*). It was written in Turkish but with [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language) script.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299]

Due to historically close ties with France, [French literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_literature) constituted the major Western influence on Ottoman literature in the latter half of the 19th century. As a result, many of the same movements prevalent in France during this period had Ottoman equivalents; in the developing Ottoman prose tradition, for instance, the influence of [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) can be seen during the Tanzimat period, and that of the [Realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)) and [Naturalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalism_\(literature\)) movements in subsequent periods; in the poetic tradition, on the other hand, the influence of the [Symbolist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(movement\)) and [Parnassian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parnassianism) movements was paramount.

Many of the writers in the Tanzimat period wrote in several different genres simultaneously. This diversity was, in part, due to Tanzimat writers' wish to disseminate as much of the new literature as possible, in the hopes that it would contribute to a revitalisation of Ottoman [social structures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_structure).[^cite_note-300]

### Media

Main article: [Media of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_the_Ottoman_Empire)

The media of the Ottoman Empire was diverse, with newspapers and journals published in languages including [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_\(language\)),[^cite_note-BaruhMusnik-301] [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_\(language\)),[^cite_note-StraussConstp32-302] and [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_\(language\)).[^cite_note-Syed-2011-270] Many of these publications were centred in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople),[^cite_note-Kendallp339-303] but there were also French-language newspapers produced in [Beirut](37428.md), [Salonika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salonika), and [Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna).[^cite_note-StraussLanguagep122-304] Non-Muslim ethnic minorities in the empire used French as a [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) and used French-language publications,[^cite_note-BaruhMusnik-301] while some provincial newspapers were published in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic).[^cite_note-StraussConstp25-305] The use of French in the media persisted until the [end of the empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) in 1923 and for a few years thereafter in the [Republic of Turkey](11125639.md).[^cite_note-BaruhMusnik-301]

### Architecture

Main article: [Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture)



[Süleymaniye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleymaniye_Mosque) in Istanbul, designed by [Mimar Sinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Sinan) in the 16th century and a major example of the [classical Ottoman style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Ottoman_architecture)

The architecture of the empire developed from earlier [Seljuk Turkish architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_Seljuk_architecture), with influences from [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture) and [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_architecture) architecture and other architectural traditions in the Middle East.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-Bloom-2009-307][^cite_note-Freely-2011-308] [Early Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Ottoman_architecture) experimented with multiple building types over the course of the 13th to 15th centuries, progressively evolving into the [classical Ottoman style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Ottoman_architecture) of the 16th and 17th centuries, which was also strongly influenced by the [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia).[^cite_note-Freely-2011-308][^cite_note-Goodwin-309] The most important architect of the Classical period is [Mimar Sinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Sinan), whose major works include the [Şehzade Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eehzade_Mosque), [Süleymaniye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleymaniye_Mosque), and [Selimiye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selimiye_Mosque,_Edirne).[^cite_note-AgostonMasters20102-310][^cite_note-Grove_encycl-Ottoman-311] The second half of the 16th century also saw the apogee of some [types of decoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_decoration), most notably in the production of [Iznik tiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iznik_pottery).[^cite_note-312]



[Sebil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebil) (water distribution kiosk) of the [Mihrişah Sultan Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihri%C5%9Fah_Sultan_Complex) in Istanbul, from the late 18th century, an example of the [Ottoman Baroque style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Baroque_style)

Beginning in the 18th century, Ottoman architecture was opened to external influences, particularly from [Baroque architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture) in Western Europe, which eventually gave rise to the [Ottoman Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Baroque_architecture) style that emerged in the 1740s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERüstem201921–70_(see_chapters_1_and_2_generally)-313] The [Nuruosmaniye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuruosmaniye_Mosque) is one of the most important examples from this period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreely2011355-314][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuban2010526-315] During the 19th century, influences from Western Europe became more prominent, brought in by architects such as those from the [Balyan family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balyan_family).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreely2011393-316] [Empire style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_style) and [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) motifs were introduced and a trend towards [eclecticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclecticism_in_architecture) was evident in many types of buildings, such as the [Dolmabaçe Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmabah%C3%A7e_Palace).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuban2010605–606-317] The last decades of the Ottoman Empire also saw the development of a new architectural style called neo-Ottoman or Ottoman [revivalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revivalism_\(architecture\)), also known as the [First National Architectural Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_national_architectural_movement),[^cite_note-Bloom-2009a-318] by architects such as [Mimar Kemaleddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Kemaleddin) and [Vedat Tek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedat_Tek).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreely2011393-316]

Ottoman dynastic patronage was concentrated in the historic capitals of Bursa, Edirne, and Istanbul (Constantinople), as well as in several other important administrative centres such as [Amasya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amasya) and [Manisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manisa). It was in these centres that most important developments in Ottoman architecture occurred and that the most monumental Ottoman architecture can be found.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuban2010679-319] Major religious monuments were typically architectural complexes, known as a *[külliye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%BClliye)*, that had multiple components providing different services or amenities. In addition to a mosque, these could include a [madrasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa), a [hammam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammam), an [imaret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imaret), a [sebil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebil_\(fountain\)), a market, a [caravanserai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravanserai), a [primary school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuttab), or others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuban2010-320] These complexes were governed and managed with the help of a *[vakıf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vak%C4%B1f)* agreement (Arabic *waqf*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuban2010-320] Ottoman constructions were still abundant in Anatolia and in the Balkans (Rumelia), but in the more distant Middle Eastern and North African provinces older [Islamic architectural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture) styles continued to hold strong influence and were sometimes blended with Ottoman styles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuban2010571–596-321][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlairBloom1995251-322]

### Decorative arts

Further information: [Ottoman illumination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_illumination) and [Ottoman miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature)



[Ottoman miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature) lost its function with the Westernisation of Ottoman culture.

The tradition of [Ottoman miniatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature), painted to illustrate manuscripts or used in dedicated albums, was heavily influenced by the [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_miniature) art form, though it also included elements of the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_art) tradition of [illumination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illuminated_manuscript) and painting.[^cite_note-323] A Greek academy of painters, the *Nakkashane-i-Rum*, was established in the [Topkapi Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkap%C4%B1_Palace) in the 15th century, while early in the following century a similar Persian academy, the *Nakkashane-i-Irani*, was added. [Surname-i Hümayun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname-i_H%C3%BCmayun) (Imperial Festival Books) were albums that commemorated celebrations in the Ottoman Empire in pictorial and textual detail.

[Ottoman illumination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_illumination) covers non-figurative painted or drawn decorative art in books or on sheets in *[muraqqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muraqqa)* or albums, as opposed to the figurative images of the [Ottoman miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature). It was a part of the Ottoman Book Arts together with the Ottoman miniature (*taswir*), calligraphy (*hat*), [Islamic calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calligraphy), bookbinding (*cilt*) and [paper marbling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_marbling) (*ebru*). In the Ottoman Empire, [illuminated and illustrated manuscripts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illuminated_manuscript) were commissioned by the Sultan or the administrators of the court. In Topkapi Palace, these manuscripts were created by the artists working in *Nakkashane*, the atelier of the miniature and illumination artists. Both religious and non-religious books could be illuminated. Also, sheets for albums *levha* consisted of illuminated calligraphy (*hat*) of *[tughra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tughra)*, religious texts, verses from poems or proverbs, and purely decorative drawings.

The art of carpet [weaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving) was particularly significant in the Ottoman Empire, carpets having an immense importance both as decorative furnishings, rich in religious and other symbolism and as a practical consideration, as it was customary to remove one's shoes in living quarters.[^cite_note-foroqhi-324] The weaving of such carpets originated in the [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomad) cultures of central Asia (carpets being an easily transportable form of furnishing), and eventually spread to the settled societies of Anatolia. Turks used carpets, rugs, and [kilims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilim) not just on the floors of a room but also as a hanging on walls and doorways, where they provided additional insulation. They were also commonly donated to mosques, which often amassed large collections of them.[^cite_note-foroqhip153-325]

### Music and performing arts

Further information: [Ottoman music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_music)



Musicians and dancers entertaining the crowds, from the [Surname-i Hümayun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname-i_H%C3%BCmayun), 1720

[Ottoman classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_music) was an important part of the education of the Ottoman elite. A number of the Ottoman sultans have accomplished musicians and composers themselves, such as [Selim III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_III), whose compositions are often still performed today. Ottoman classical music arose largely from a confluence of [Byzantine music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_music), [Armenian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Armenia), [Arabic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_music), and [Persian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_traditional_music). Compositionally, it is organised around rhythmic units called [usul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usul_\(music\)), which are somewhat similar to [metre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre_\(music\)) in Western music, and [melodic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melody) units called [makam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_makam), which bear some resemblance to Western [musical modes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mode_\(music\)).

The [instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_instrument) used are a mixture of Anatolian and Central Asian instruments (the saz, the [bağlama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%9Flama), the [kemence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemenche)), other Middle Eastern instruments (the [ud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oud), the [tanbur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanbur), the [kanun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanun_\(instrument\)), the [ney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ney)), and—later in the tradition—Western instruments (the violin and the piano). Because of a geographic and cultural divide between the capital and other areas, two broadly distinct styles of music arose in the Ottoman Empire: Ottoman classical music and folk music. In the provinces, several different kinds of [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music) were created. The most dominant regions with their distinguished musical styles are Balkan-Thracian Türküs, North-Eastern ([Laz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laz_people)) Türküs, Aegean Türküs, Central Anatolian Türküs, Eastern Anatolian Türküs, and Caucasian Türküs. Some of the distinctive styles were: [Janissary music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_military_band), [Roma music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_music), [Belly dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belly_dance), [Turkish folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_folk_music).

The traditional [shadow play](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_play) called [Karagöz and Hacivat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karag%C3%B6z_and_Hacivat) was widespread throughout the Ottoman Empire and featured characters representing all of the major ethnic and social groups in that culture.[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] It was performed by a single puppet master, who voiced all of the characters, and accompanied by [tambourine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambourine) (*def*). Its origins are obscure, deriving perhaps from an older Egyptian tradition, or possibly from an Asian source.

### Cuisine

Main article: [Ottoman cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_cuisine)



Turkish women baking bread, 1790

[Ottoman cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_cuisine) is the cuisine of the capital, Constantinople ([Istanbul](3391396.md)), and the regional capital cities, where the melting pot of cultures created a common cuisine that most of the population regardless of ethnicity shared. This diverse cuisine was honed in the Imperial Palace's kitchens by chefs brought from certain parts of the Empire to create and experiment with different ingredients. The creations of the Ottoman Palace's kitchens filtered to the population, for instance through [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan) events, and through the cooking at the [Yalıs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yal%C4%B1) of the [Pashas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasha), and from there on spread to the rest of the population.

Much of the cuisine of former Ottoman territories today is descended from a shared Ottoman cuisine, especially [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_cuisine), and including [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_cuisine), [Balkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_cuisine), [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_cuisine), and [Middle Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Eastern_cuisine) cuisines.[^cite_note-Fragner-328]

### Sports



Members of [Beşiktaş J.K.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F_J.K.) in 1903

The main sports Ottomans were engaged in were [Turkish wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling_in_Turkey), hunting, [Turkish archery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_archery), horseback riding, [equestrian javelin throw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jereed), arm wrestling, and swimming. European model sports clubs were formed with the spreading popularity of [football](10568.md) matches in 19th-century Constantinople. The leading clubs, according to timeline, were [Beşiktaş Gymnastics Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F_J.K.) (1903), [Galatasaray Sports Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_S.K.) (1905), [Fenerbahçe Sports Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_S.K.) (1907), [MKE Ankaragücü (formerly Turan Sanatkarangücü)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MKE_Ankarag%C3%BCc%C3%BC) (1910) in Constantinople. Football clubs were formed in other provinces too, such as [Karşıyaka Sports Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kar%C5%9F%C4%B1yaka_S.K.) (1912), [Altay Sports Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altay_S.K.) (1914) and Turkish Fatherland Football Club (later [Ülküspor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%9Clk%C3%BCspor)) (1914) of [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir).

## Science and technology

Main article: [Science and technology in the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_the_Ottoman_Empire)



The [Constantinople observatory of Taqi ad-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople_observatory_of_Taqi_ad-Din) in 1577

Over the course of Ottoman history, the Ottomans managed to build a large collection of libraries complete with translations of books from other cultures, as well as original manuscripts.[^cite_note-Ágoston_and_Alan_Masters583-74] A great part of this desire for local and foreign manuscripts arose in the 15th century. [Sultan Mehmet II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II) ordered [Georgios Amiroutzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Amiroutzes), a Greek scholar from [Trabzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trabzon), to translate and make available to Ottoman educational institutions the geography book of [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy). Another example is [Ali Qushji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Qushji) – an [astronomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world), [mathematician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world) and [physicist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world) originally from [Samarkand](51491.md) – who became a professor in two madrasas and influenced Ottoman circles as a result of his writings and the activities of his students, even though he only spent two or three years in Constantinople before his death.[^cite_note-329]

[Taqi al-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqi_ad-Din_Muhammad_ibn_Ma'ruf) built the [Constantinople observatory of Taqi ad-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople_observatory_of_Taqi_ad-Din) in 1577, where he carried out observations until 1580. He calculated the [eccentricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_eccentricity) of the Sun's orbit and the annual motion of the [apogee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apsis).[^cite_note-Tekeli-330] However, the observatory's primary purpose was almost certainly [astrological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrology) rather than astronomical, leading to its destruction in 1580 due to the rise of a clerical faction that opposed its use for that purpose.[^cite_note-331] He also experimented with [steam power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine) in [Ottoman Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_Eyalet) in 1551, when he described a [steam jack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roasting_jack) driven by a rudimentary [steam turbine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_turbine).[^cite_note-332]



*Girl Reciting the Qurān* (*Kuran Okuyan Kız*), an 1880 painting by the Ottoman polymath [Osman Hamdi Bey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_Hamdi_Bey), whose works often showed women engaged in educational activities

[^cite_note-333]
